Chi-square and normal inference in high-dimensional multi-task regression
Pierre C. Bellec and Gabriel Romon

Abstract
The paper proposes chi-square and normal inference methodologies for the unknown coefficient matrix 𝑩 ∗ \boldsymbol{B}^{*} of size p × T p\times T in a multi-task linear model with p p covariates, T T tasks and n n observations under a row-sparse assumption on 𝑩 ∗ \boldsymbol{B}^{*} . The row-sparsity s s , dimension p p and number of tasks T T are allowed to grow with n n . In the high-dimensional regime p ⋙ n p\ggg n , in order to leverage the row-sparsity [ 33 , 42 ] , the multi-task Lasso is considered. We build upon the multi-task Lasso with a de-biasing scheme to correct for the bias induced by the penalty. The de-biasing scheme requires the introduction of a new data-driven object, coined the interaction matrix, that captures the effective correlations between noise vector and residuals on different tasks. The interaction matrix is symmetric positive semi-definite, of size T × T T\times T and can be computed efficiently. The interaction matrix lets us derive asymptotic normal and asymptotic χ T 2 \chi^{2}_{T} results under general Gaussian design and the rate condition s ​ T + s ​ log ⁡ ( p / s ) / n → 0 {sT+s\log(p/s)}/{n}\to 0 which corresponds to consistency in Frobenius norm of the multi-task Lasso. These asymptotic distribution results yield valid confidence intervals for single entries of 𝑩 ∗ \boldsymbol{B}^{*} and valid confidence ellipsoids for single rows of 𝑩 ∗ \boldsymbol{B}^{*} . If the covariance of the design is unknown, a modification of the multi-task de-biasing scheme using the nodewise Lasso provides comparable confidence intervals and confidence ellipsoids for the j j -th row of 𝑩 ∗ \boldsymbol{B}^{*} , provided that the j j -th column of the precision matrix 𝚺 − 1 \boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1} is sufficiently sparse. While previous proposals in grouped-variables regression require row-sparsity s ≲ n s\lesssim\sqrt{n} up to constants depending on T T and logarithmic factors in ( n , p ) (n,p) for unknown 𝚺 \boldsymbol{\Sigma} , the de-biasing scheme using the interaction matrix provides confidence intervals and χ T 2 \chi^{2}_{T} confidence ellipsoids under the conditions min ⁡ ( T 2 , log 8 ⁡ p ) / n → 0 {\min(T^{2},\log^{8}p)}/{n}\to 0 and s ​ T + s ​ log ⁡ ( p / s ) + ‖ 𝚺 − 1 ​ 𝒆 j ‖ 0 ​ log ⁡ p n → 0 , min ⁡ ( s , ‖ 𝚺 − 1 ​ 𝒆 j ‖ 0 ) n ​ [ T + log ⁡ ( p / s ) ] ​ log ⁡ p → 0 , \frac{sT+s\log(p/s)+\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{0}\log p}{n}\to 0,\quad\frac{\min(s,\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{0})}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{[T+\log(p/s)]\log p}\to 0, allowing for row-sparsity s ⋙ n s\ggg\sqrt{n} when ‖ 𝚺 − 1 ​ 𝒆 j ‖ 0 ​ T ⋘ n \|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{0}\sqrt{T}\lll\sqrt{n} up to logarithmic factors.



1
Introduction

1.1
Model

We consider a multi-task linear regression model with
T
tasks,
with
n
i.i.d. observations
(\boldsymbol{x}_{i},y^{{(1)}}_{i},...,y^{{(T)}}_{i})
,
where
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is a random feature vector and

y^{{(1)}}_{i},...,y^{{(T)}}_{i}
are
T
different scalar responses.
We assume that on each task
t=1,...,T
, the response
y_{i}^{{(t)}}

satisfies a linear model

(1.1)

y_{i}^{{(t)}}=\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{{(t)}}+\epsilon_{i}^{{(t)}},\qquad t=1,...,T

where
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{{(t)}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is the unknown coefficient vector
on the task
t
.
Throughout,
\boldsymbol{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
is the design matrix with
n
rows

\boldsymbol{x}_{1}^{\top},...,\boldsymbol{x}_{n}^{\top}
. The linear models (
1.1
) may be
rewritten in vector and matrix form

(1.2)

\boldsymbol{y}^{{(t)}}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{{(t)}}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{{(t)}},\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}+\boldsymbol{E}

where

\boldsymbol{y}^{{(t)}}=(y_{1}^{{(t)}},...,y_{n}^{{(t)}})^{\top}

and

{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{{(t)}}=(\epsilon_{1}^{{(t)}},...,\epsilon_{n}^{{(t)}})^{\top}

are vectors in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,

\boldsymbol{Y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times T}
is the response matrix with columns
\boldsymbol{y}^{{(1)}},...,\boldsymbol{y}^{{(T)}}
,

\boldsymbol{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times T}
is a noise matrix with columns
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{{(1)}},...,{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{{(T)}}
,
and
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}
is an unknown coefficient matrix with
columns
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{{(1)}},...,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{{(T)}}
.

Estimation of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
in the above multi-task model has been well studied
during the last decade in the high-dimensional regime where
p\ggg n
, see for instance

[
33
]
. This literature on multi-task learning suggests to use a joint convex optimization
problem over the tasks in order to estimate
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
, namely

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\boldsymbol{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}}\Big[\frac{1}{2nT}\|\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}\|_{F}^{2}+g(\boldsymbol{B})\Big]=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\boldsymbol{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}}\Big[\frac{1}{2nT}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}^{{(t)}}-\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{e}_{t})^{2}+g(\boldsymbol{B})\Big]

where
\boldsymbol{e}_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
is the
t
-th canonical basis vector,

\|\cdot\|_{F}
is the Frobenius norm of matrices and
g:{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}\to{\mathbb{R}}

is a convex penalty function. The role of the convex penalty
g
is to
promote a shared structure on the coefficient vectors
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)},...,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(T)}
.
The most common shared structure is that of row-sparsity where one assumes that only a few features are relevant across all tasks:
there is a support set

S\subset\{1,...,p\}
of small cardinality (relatively to
n,p
) such that
for every task
t=1,...,T
,
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{{(t)}}_{j}=0\iff j\notin S
.
Equivalently,
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}={\mathbf{0}}_{1\times T}
if and only if
j\notin S
,
i.e., only
|S|
rows of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
are nonzero.
In this case, the sparsity pattern encoded by
S\subset\{1,...,p\}

is shared on all tasks, and previous literature on estimation in this setting
uses a penalty proportional to the
\ell_{2,1}
norm,
g(\boldsymbol{B})=\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}
, or alternatively its Elastic-Net version

g(\boldsymbol{B})=\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}+\mu\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{F}^{2}

for non-negative tuning parameters
\lambda,\mu\geq 0
.
If the row-sparsity assumption holds and such
\ell_{2,1}
penalty is used,
estimation of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
by
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
is improved compared to estimating

\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)},...,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(T)}
separately
[
33
]
.

1.2
Noise and residuals: non-trivial correlations for non-separable
penalties

Classical multivariate statistics studies the least-squares estimate

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{(ls)}=(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Y}
,
which corresponds to
g(\cdot)=0
in the above minimization problem.
Here, the estimation on two tasks is independent,
as on the
t
-th task for
t=1,...,T
we have

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{(ls)}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}=(\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{y}^{(t)}

for the
t
-th canonical basis vector
\boldsymbol{e}_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
:
the estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{(ls)}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}
of the unknown regression vector
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}

on the
t
-th task only depends on the
t
-th response
\boldsymbol{y}^{(t)}
,
and is independent of the other responses
(\boldsymbol{y}^{(t^{\prime})})_{t^{\prime}\in\{1,...,T\}\setminus\{t\}}
. By independence, if the noise
\boldsymbol{E}
has i.i.d. mean-zero entries,
then

(1.3)

{\mathbb{E}}[{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{(t^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{(ls)})^{\top}]=\mathbf{0}_{n\times n}\qquad\forall t\neq t^{\prime},

i.e., residual and noise on two different tasks are uncorrelated.
A similar story holds for multi-task Ridge regression, which
corresponds to
g(\boldsymbol{B})=\mu\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{F}^{2}
in the above minimization
problem. The optimization problem is separable in the sense that

\displaystyle{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{(R)}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\boldsymbol{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}\|_{F}^{2}}{2nT}+\mu\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{F}^{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{(R)}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\boldsymbol{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{y}^{(t)}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{b}\|_{2}^{2}}{2nT}+\mu\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{2}^{2}

equivalently define
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{(R)}
. It follows again that
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{(R)}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}

only depends on the
t
-th response
\boldsymbol{y}^{(t)}
, and
if
\boldsymbol{E}
has i.i.d. mean-zero entries
then (
1.3
)
holds also for
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{(R)}
by independence.

The situation is more complex for non-separable penalty functions,
for instance if the penalty is proportional to the
\ell_{2,1}
norm,

g(\boldsymbol{B})=\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}

where
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is the
j
-th canonical basis vector.
The corresponding
estimator studied throughout the paper is the multi-task Lasso

(1.4)

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\boldsymbol{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}}\Big(\frac{1}{2nT}\|\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}\|_{F}^{2}+\lambda\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{2,1}\Big)\quad\text{where}\quad\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{2,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|{\boldsymbol{B}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}}\|_{2}.

The estimate
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}
of the unknown vector
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)}

on the
t
-th task depends in an intricate way on all the responses
including
(\boldsymbol{y}^{(t^{\prime})})_{t^{\prime}\in\{1,...,T\}\setminus\{t\}}
.
Note that this dependence of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}
on all responses is
purposeful: we hope to leverage a shared pattern on all tasks
(e.g., if
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
is row-sparse and a sparsity pattern
is shared by all
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},t=1,...,T

) in order to improve estimation
compared to
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{(ls)}
or
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{(R)}
.
In this case, however, (
1.3
) does not hold
and the correlation between the residual on task
t
and the noise
on task
t^{\prime}
is non-trivial. Our results below (specifically

Lemma

F.1
)
reveal that for
t,t^{\prime}\in[T]
,

\bigl((\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\boldsymbol{e}_{t}\bigr)^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{(t^{\prime})}\approx\begin{cases}\sigma^{2}(n-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}_{tt^{\prime}})&\text{ if }t=t^{\prime}\\
-\sigma^{2}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}_{tt^{\prime}}&\text{ if }t\neq t^{\prime}\end{cases}

when the noise
\boldsymbol{E}
has i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
entries
and
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}_{tt^{\prime}}
is the
(t,t^{\prime})
entry of a symmetric matrix

\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times T}
defined in
Section

2
. This matrix plays
a central role in the present paper to derive asymptotic normality
and asymptotic
\chi^{2}
results.

1.3
Confidence intervals for linear functionals of
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{{(1)}}

A first goal
of the present paper is to provide
confidence intervals for linear functionals of the
regression vector on the first task. Throughout the paper,
regarding asymptotic normality and confidence intervals,

\boldsymbol{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is a fixed direction
of interest and we wish to construct confidence intervals for
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{{(1)}}
. For instance, the direction
\boldsymbol{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
may be
of the following form.

(i)

a canonical basis vector
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
. For
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
,
the goal is to construct confidence intervals for

\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}=\beta_{j}^{{(1)}}
, the coefficient
of the
j
-th feature on the first task.
This is the classical goal in statistics where one wishes
to provide inference on the effect of the
j
-th covariate.

(ii)

a new feature vector
\boldsymbol{x}_{new}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
, that may for instance
correspond to the characteristics of a new subject whose responses

y_{new}^{{(1)}},...,y_{new}^{{(T)}}

are not known yet. The goal is to provide a confidence interval
for
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}
which corresponds to the expected response
of
Y_{new}
conditionally on the feature vector
\boldsymbol{x}_{new}
.

We stress here that the first task (
t=1
) has a special role: the unknown
parameter
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}
only involves the first unknown coefficient vector
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}

and not the other coefficient vectors
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(t)},t=2,...,T
.
If a single linear model
\boldsymbol{y}^{{(1)}}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{{(1)}}

is observed, the construction of confidence intervals for
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}

has been extensively studied. Most related to the present paper,

[
56
,
51
,
26
,
27
]
initially provided
methodologies
for de-biasing (or de-sparsifying) the Lasso for construction of confidence intervals
in a canonical basis direction
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
for sparsity
s\lesssim\sqrt{n}/\log p
,

[
28
]
extended the sparsity requirement to
s\lesssim n/(\log p)^{2}
,

[
58
,
11
,
13
,
14
,
59
,
5
]
studied estimation and construction of confidence intervals
in dense direction
\boldsymbol{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
,
and
[
6
]
extended the de-biasing methodologies to arbitrary convex
penalties.

Of course, one could throw away the responses
\boldsymbol{y}^{{(2)}},...,\boldsymbol{y}^{{(T)}}
and use only the response
\boldsymbol{y}^{{(1)}}
with the aforementioned
methodologies, since our goal is to construct
confidence intervals for
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}
. However, throwing away
the responses on tasks
2,...,T
should intuitively lead to information loss
and is not desirable.

1.4
Asymptotic
\chi^{2}
results and confidence ellipsoids
for rows of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}

The second goal of the paper is to develop
confidence ellipsoids for whole rows of the unknown matrix

\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
. The
j
-th row of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
is
the vector
(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{T}

where
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is the
j
-th canonical vector.
Given a confidence level
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
a confidence ellipsoid for
(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}

is a subset
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{T}

constructed from the data such that

{\mathbb{P}}\bigl((\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\bigr)\geq 1-\alpha-o(1)

where
o(1)
converges to 0 as
n\to+\infty
.
Ideally, the confidence ellipsoid enjoys the exact
nominal coverage probability
1-\alpha
asymptotically in the sense
that

(1.5)

\big|{\mathbb{P}}\bigl((\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\bigr)-(1-\alpha)\big|\to 0

as
n\to+\infty
.
Note that one could also consider confidence sets
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}

that are not ellipsoids (e.g., hyperrectangles);
we focus here on ellipsoids as they are the natural confidence sets
stemming from
\chi^{2}
-distributed pivotal quantities.
As in classical multivariate statistics,
an advantage of confidence ellipsoids is that
they provide simultaneous confidence intervals
for every direction
\boldsymbol{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
, that is,

{\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\forall\boldsymbol{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T},\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\boldsymbol{b}\in\{\boldsymbol{b}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{u}\in\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\}\bigr)\to 1-\alpha

when (
1.5
) holds
and
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
is closed and convex.

Such a confidence ellipsoid allows to perform hypothesis tests of

(1.6)

H_{0}:(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}=\mathbf{0}_{T\times 1}\qquad\text{ against }\qquad H_{1}:\|(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}\geq\rho,

where the null hypothesis corresponds to the signal
\boldsymbol{Y}

being independent of the
j
-th feature
\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
,
and
\rho>0
is a separation radius.
If a single task is observed (
T=1
), it is impossible
to distinguish between the null
\beta_{j}=0

and the alternative
\beta_{j}\neq 0
with constant type I and type II
errors unless
|\beta_{j}|\geq c\sigma n^{-1/2}
for some constant
c>0
.
This follows by noting that the total variation distance
between
\boldsymbol{y}^{H_{0}}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{H_{0}}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}

and
\boldsymbol{y}^{H_{1}}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{H_{1}}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
converges to
0

if
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{H_{0}},\boldsymbol{\beta}^{H_{1}}
are the same
except on coordinate
j
where
|\beta_{j}^{H_{0}}-\beta_{j}^{H_{1}}|=a_{n}
with

a_{n}=o(\sigma n^{-1/2})
,
\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|^{2}/n\asymp 1

and
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{n}(\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{I}_{n\times n})
,
for instance
by Pinsker’s inequality and a standard bound on the Kullback Leibler
divergence of two multivariate normals.
If several tasks are observed as in the setting of interest here,
we will see that it is possible to perform
the hypothesis test (
1.6
) in situations where
all nonzero coefficients of
(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
are of order

o(\sigma n^{-1/2})
, i.e., of indistinguishable order
when a single task is observed.

If asymptotic normality results are available for each
of the
T
individual
coefficients of
(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
(for instance
such as those described in the previous
subsection), a natural strategy
to construct confidence ellipsoids is to sum the square
of the
T
asymptotically normal random variables
and hope that the resulting sum has approximately the
\chi^{2}

distribution with
T
degrees-of-freedom.
However, throughout the paper the number of tasks
T

is allowed to grow to infinity with
n
which
results in some challenges regarding this strategy,
as pointed out by
[
41
]
.
For the sake of illustrating the resulting difficulty,
assume that we have established the asymptotic normality of
T

pivotal random variables
U_{1},...,U_{T}
by
proving decompositions of the form
U_{t}=(\hat{\sigma}/\sigma)Z_{t}+B_{t}

where
Z_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and the convergence in probability

\hat{\sigma}/\sigma\smash{\xrightarrow{{\mathbb{P}}}}1

and
B_{t}\smash{\xrightarrow{{\mathbb{P}}}}0
hold,
so that Slutsky’s theorem ensures that the pivotal quantities
are asymptotically normal
with
U_{t}\smash{\xrightarrow{d}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
Denoting by
\chi^{2}_{T}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}^{2}
, summing the squares
of the pivotal quantities
and applying the triangle inequality for the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{T}

yields

(1.7)

\textstyle\big|\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}U_{t}^{2}}-\sqrt{\chi^{2}_{T}}\big|\leq|\hat{\sigma}/\sigma-1|\sqrt{\chi^{2}_{T}}+\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}B_{t}^{2}}.

While
{\mathbb{E}}[(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}]
is of order
\sqrt{T}
,
the variance and quantiles of
(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}
are of
constant order
(specifically,

{\mathbb{P}}((\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}-\sqrt{T}\leq z_{\alpha}/\sqrt{2})\to 1-\alpha

holds by (
F.17
) below,
and
\text{Var}[(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}]\to 1/2
by
[
25
]
).
This implies that a sufficient condition that ensures
that
(\sum_{t=1}^{T}U_{t}^{2})^{1/2}
and
(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}

asymptotically share the same quantiles is that

\sum_{t=1}^{T}B_{t}^{2}\smash{\xrightarrow{{\mathbb{P}}}}0

and

\sqrt{T}|\hat{\sigma}/\sigma-1|\smash{\xrightarrow{{\mathbb{P}}}}0
.
While
B_{1}\smash{\xrightarrow{{\mathbb{P}}}}0
and
\hat{\sigma}/\sigma\smash{\xrightarrow{{\mathbb{P}}}}1
are sufficient
to grant asymptotic normality
for
U_{1}
on the first task,
the conditions

\sqrt{T}|\hat{\sigma}/\sigma-1|\smash{\xrightarrow{{\mathbb{P}}}}0

and

(\sum_{t=1}^{T}B_{t}^{2})^{1/2}\smash{\xrightarrow{{\mathbb{P}}}}0

are much more stringent
as they involve the number of tasks
T
.

1.5
Asymptotics and assumptions

We will derive asymptotic normality and asymptotic
\chi^{2}_{T}

results for a sequence of multi-task regression
problems of increasing dimensions. For each
n
,
we consider the multi-task linear model (
1.2
) and
and the multi-task Lasso estimate
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
in (
1.4
)
where
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
,
the number of tasks
T
, dimension
p
, tuning parameter

\lambda
and row-sparsity
s
are all functions of
n
.
The dependence in
n
is implicit and will be omitted to avoid notational burden.
We will assume that the sequence of regression problems satisfies the following.

Assumption 1.1
.

(i)

\boldsymbol{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
is a Gaussian design matrix with i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}_{p}(\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
rows;

(ii)

\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}
is a row-sparse unknown matrix with at most
s
nonzero rows;

(iii)

\boldsymbol{E}
is a Gaussian noise matrix with i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
entries;

(iv)

\{s,n,T,p\}
are positive and satisfy
\frac{s}{n}(T+\log\frac{p}{s})\to 0
and
n\leq p
,
this implies
\frac{s}{p}\vee\frac{T}{n}\to 0
;

(v)

The spectrum of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is bounded:
C_{\min}\leq\phi_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\leq\phi_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\leq C_{\max}
for some constants
0<C_{\min}\leq C_{\max}

which are independent of
n,p,s,T
;

(vi)

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
satisfies
\max_{j=1,...,p}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{jj}\leq 1
;

(vii)

For two constants
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}>0
,
the tuning parameter
\lambda
in (
1.4
)
is given by

(1.8)

\lambda=(1+\eta_{2})\lambda_{0},\quad\text{where}\quad\lambda_{0}=\Big(\max_{j=1,...,p}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{jj}^{1/2}\Big)\frac{\sigma(1+\eta_{1})}{\sqrt{nT}}\Big(1+\sqrt{(2/T)\log(p/s)}\Big).

1.6
Related literature

For integers
\bar{n},\bar{p}\geq 1
, the multi-task setting
above bears resemblance with the single-response
linear model of the form

(1.9)

\bar{\boldsymbol{y}}=\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\bar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}

where
\boldsymbol{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\bar{n}}
,
\bar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\bar{n}}
,

\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\bar{n}\times\bar{p}}
,
and the features
\{1,...,\bar{p}\}
are partitioned into
p
groups
with equal sizes. Indeed, with
\bar{p}=pT
,
\bar{n}=nT

and by vectorizing the matrices in (
1.1
),
our multi-task setting is in one-to-one correspondence
with the single-response linear model (
1.9
)
with
\bar{\boldsymbol{y}}=\vec(\boldsymbol{Y})
,
\bar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=\vec(\boldsymbol{E})
,

\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}
block diagonal with
T
blocks each equal to
\boldsymbol{X}
,
and the partition
(G_{1},...,G_{p})
of

\{1,...,\bar{p}\}
into
p
groups is given by

G_{j}=\{j+(t-1)p,t=1,...,T\}
.
With this correspondence, the estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
is the group Lasso

\hat{\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\bar{p}}}\|\bar{\boldsymbol{y}}-\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}\|^{2}/(2\bar{n})+\lambda\|\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}\|_{2,1}
where

\|\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}\|_{2,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|\bar{\boldsymbol{b}}_{G_{j}}\|_{2}
.
Inference for grouped variables in a single-response linear model
(
1.9
) focuses on estimation, hypothesis tests
or confidence sets
for the vector
\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{G_{j}}
for a group
G_{j}\subset\{1,...,\bar{p}\}

of interest.
In the single task setting (
1.9
) with grouped variables,

[
41
]
extends the de-biasing methodology
in
[
56
,
51
]

to inference for grouped variables and provides
\chi^{2}
asymptotic
distribution results.
The paper
[
41
]
already describes some challenges
of chi-square inference in high-dimension (cf. the discussion after
(
1.7
)); the multi-task problem
of the present paper shares some of these challenges,
however our approach and proofs have no overlap with
that of
[
41
]
.
The papers
[
47
,
52
]
give a different
extension of the de-biasing methodology of
[
56
,
51
]

to the group setting, again based on the group Lasso, but
here by estimation of the inverse covariance matrix restricted
to the group of interest with a multi-task estimator
penalized by the nuclear norm.
False Discovery Rate control in single-task linear models
with grouped variables has been studied in

[
12
]
with a group SLOPE estimator.
Under weak assumptions (in particular, no assumption on
\boldsymbol{X}
),

[
37
]
provides an approach
to inference for grouped variables, although the resulting confidence
regions are conservative.
The papers
[
34
,
35
]

study group inference in a sequence rejection fashion
when the groups are hierarchically ordered.
Bootstrap methods based on the group Lasso are studied in

[
57
]
, without trying to remove the bias.
The paper
[
20
]
develops
conservative inference methods for
quantities of the form
(\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{G_{j}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{A}\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{G_{j}}

for a group
G_{j}\subset[p]
of interest and a given positive definite matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|G_{j}|\times|G_{j}|}
, based on the quadratic program
de-biasing methodology given in
[
56
,
26
]
.
Finally,
[
6
]
introduces a degrees-of-freedom adjustment
for the group Lasso to perform inference on a single coordinate or linear form
of the unknown regression vector in (
1.9
).

Some papers focus on estimation and inference in the multi-task
model (
1.2
).
The papers
[
52
,
8
]
study
multi-task models of the form (
1.2
) where
the noise
\boldsymbol{E}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times T}
has i.i.d. rows, and the entries
within each row are correlated.
A multi-task extension of the square-root Lasso is developed to
concurrently estimate
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
and the correlations in the noise
\boldsymbol{E}
.
Such results on estimating the correlations of
the entries in
\boldsymbol{E}
are useful to de-bias the group Lasso
in the single-task model
[
52
]
.
Support recovery through bounds on the group norm

\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{2,\infty}=\sup_{j\in[p]}\|\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}

is studied in
[
36
]
under a mutual incoherence
assumption on
\boldsymbol{X}
.
The mutual incoherence assumption
requires a row-sparsity level

s\lesssim\sqrt{n}
if
\boldsymbol{X}
has i.i.d. entries.
Closest to the setup and goals of the present paper,

[
17
]
extends the de-biasing
methodology of
[
56
,
51
]
to the multi-task setting,
using the nodewise Lasso to estimate a column of the precision matrix
of the design. This approach requires row-sparsity of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
of order

s\lesssim\sqrt{n}
up to logarithmic factors.
Although our approach also involves the nodewise Lasso to estimate
columns of the precision matrix, the de-biasing methodology significantly
differs from
[
17
]

and cannot be seen as a straightforward extension of
[
56
,
51
]
: our approach requires the introduction of a data-driven
symmetric matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
of size
T\times T
which captures
the interactions between the residuals on different tasks.
Introduction of this novel object lets us significantly relax
the requirement on the row-sparsity of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
while obtaining
normal and
\chi^{2}_{T}
inference results, that are proved
to be non-conservative under some assumption on
T,s,n,p
.

1.7
Adjustments in high-dimensional inference

In single-task models, recent literature on high-dimensional
inference has highlighted the necessity to adjust
classical inference principles with scalar adjustments.
To describe such adjustments
consider a single-task linear model
\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}

with
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
, Gaussian noise
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{n}(\mathbf{0},\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{n\times n})

and
\boldsymbol{X}
with i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}_{p}(\mathbf{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
rows,
where an initial estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}{}^{(init)}

is available. If one is interested in confidence
intervals for the projection
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}

in some direction
\boldsymbol{a}
normalized with

\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}=1
,
a 1-step MLE correction
in direction
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}

[
54
]
,
i.e., maximizing the likelihood over the one-dimensional
model
\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{(init)}+u\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{a},u\in{\mathbb{R}}\}

yields the corrected estimate

(1.10)

\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(init)}+\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{(init)})\|\boldsymbol{z}_{0}\|_{2}^{-2}

where
\boldsymbol{z}_{0}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}

when
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}=1
;
and the direction
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}
is the one that maximizes the Fisher information

[
54
]
.
(Since
\|\boldsymbol{z}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}\sim\chi^{2}_{n}
concentrates around
n
,
we allow ourselves to replace
\|\boldsymbol{z}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}
by
n
in (
1.10
)
in this informal discussion).
In high dimensions, this general principle requires
a modification that accounts for the degrees-of-freedom
of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}{}^{(init)}
:

[
27
,
5
]
for the Lasso
and
[
6
]
for general penalty suggest
to amplify the correction with the degrees-of-freedom adjustment
(1-\hat{\mathsf{df}}/n)^{-1}
and to use the estimate

(1.11)

\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(init)}+(1-\hat{\mathsf{df}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{(init)})n^{-1}

instead of (
1.10
). If
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}{}^{(init)}
is the Lasso,
the adjustment
(1-\hat{\mathsf{df}}/n)^{-1}
is required for efficiency for large
sparsity levels
[
5
]
.
For the Lasso, the data-driven adjustment
(1-\hat{\mathsf{df}}/n)^{-1}
may be replaced
by a deterministic scalar adjustment, i.e.,

(1.12)

\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(init)}+(1-\delta^{-1}s_{*})^{-1}\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{(init)})n^{-1}

where
\delta=n/p
and
s_{*}
is the scalar parameter obtained
after solving the system of two equations with two unknowns
in
[
40
, Proposition 3.1]
.
The correspondence between
\hat{\mathsf{df}}/n
and
s_{*}
can be seen
in
[
40
, Theorem F.1]

or
[
16
, Section 3.3]
.
This system of two nonlinear equations first appeared in

[
1
]
for the Lasso and can be extended
to permutation invariant penalty functions (see
[
15
]
and the references therein)
and robust M-estimators
[
50
]
.

We are not aware of previous proposals to study
such high-dimensional adjustments in the multi-task setting,
e.g., by extending the data-driven adjustment in
(
1.11
) or the deterministic one in
(
1.12
).
One goal of the paper is to fill this gap.

1.8
Contributions

To summarize
Sections

1.3
and
1.4
, the inferential goals of
the paper are twofold:

(i)

To construct valid confidence intervals for a linear functional

\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}
of the unknown coefficient on the first task,
by leveraging responses on all tasks simultaneously.

(ii)

To construct valid confidence ellipsoids for rows
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1\times T}

of the unknown coefficient matrix
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
,
for instance to provide hypothesis tests on the nullity of
the
j
-th row of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
, or equivalently testing
that the signal does not depend on the
j
-th covariate.

In order to achieve these statistical goals, we introduce
a new object, the data-driven symmetric matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times T}
.
Introduction of the matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
is
key to equip the estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
with the inference capabilities (i)
and (ii) above, as the theory and simulations of the next sections will show.
This data-driven matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
generalizes, to the multi-task
setting, the effective degrees-of-freedom
and other scalar adjustments in single-task linear models discussed
in the previous subsection. Since
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
is symmetric,
T(T+1)/2
scalar
adjustments are necessary in the multi-task setting and that number
of adjustments
is unbounded if
T\to+\infty
as a function of
n
. The fact that
a growing, unbounded number of scalar adjustments would be necessary
to achieve the above inference capabilities in the multi-task
setting was surprising—at least to us—, since existing works
on adjustments in high-dimensional statistics so far
only require a bounded number of scalar adjustments.

The paper also includes contributions related to the performance
of the multi-task estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
in (
1.4
).
We improve the logarithmic dependence in tuning parameter
\lambda

and the known upper bounds on
\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}
and
\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}

compared to
[
33
]
. We also develop tools to
show that the random matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
enjoys a multi-task
Restricted Eigenvalue (RE) condition from
[
9
]
.
Although the single-task
case follows in a straightforward manner from Gordon’s
escape through a mesh theorem (e.g.,
[
44
]
),
the multi-task version of the RE condition for the random matrix
\boldsymbol{X}

requires different tools.

1.9
Organization

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
The next section summarizes notation.

Section

2
describes a new quantity, the interaction matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}

that plays a major role in our estimates and confidence intervals.

Section

3.1
constructs confidence intervals
for
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}
when the covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
of the design
is known.

Section

3.2
extends these results and methodologies
when
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is unknown.

Section

4
develops confidence ellipsoids for rows of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
.

Section

5
provides an efficient way of computing the interaction matrix.

Section

6
presents numerical experiments that corroborate
our theoretical findings.
The proofs are deferred to appendices and some intuition
behind the main technical argument is given in

Section

A
.

1.10
Notation

Throughout the paper, the linear model vector and matrix notation
(
1.2
) holds.

T
,
p
and
s
are all non-decreasing functions of
n
. In all the displays of convergence (e.g.,
\to
,
\lim
,
o(\cdot)
,
O(\cdot)
), we implicitly mean that
n
goes to
\infty
.
Convergence in distribution and in probability are denoted by
\smash{\xrightarrow{d}}
and
\smash{\xrightarrow{{\mathbb{P}}}}
.

Estimators of the unknown
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
are denoted by

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
.
For any real
a
,
a_{+}=\max(0,a)
and
[k]=\{1,...,k\}
for any integer
k
,
e.g.,
[n],[p],[T]
.
We use indices
i,i^{\prime},i_{1},i_{2},...
to sum or loop
over
[n]
(i.e., over the
n
observations),
indices
t,t^{\prime},t_{1},t_{2},...
to sum or loop over
[T]
(i.e., over the
T
tasks),
indices
j,j^{\prime},j_{1},j_{2},...
to sum or loop over
[p]
(i.e., the
p
covariates).
The vectors
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
,
\boldsymbol{e}_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
,
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
denote the canonical basis
vector of the corresponding index; the size of such canonical vector
will be made explicit if it is not clear from context.
The identity matrices of sizes
p\times p
,

n\times n
,
T\times T
are
\boldsymbol{I}_{p\times p},\boldsymbol{I}_{n\times n}
and
\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}

respectively
and
{\mathbf{0}}_{k\times q}
is the zero matrix with
k
rows and
q
columns.

For any
q\geq 1
,
\|\cdot\|_{q}
is the
\ell_{q}
-norm of vector,
e.g.,
\|\cdot\|_{2}
is the Euclidean norm.
For any matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
,
\|\boldsymbol{M}\|_{F}
is the Frobenius norm
and
\|\boldsymbol{M}\|_{op}=\sup_{\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}=1}\|\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}
the operator norm, also known as the spectral norm.
If
\boldsymbol{M}
is symmetric,
\phi_{\min}(\boldsymbol{M})
(resp.
\phi_{\max}(\boldsymbol{M})
) denotes the smallest (resp. largest) eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{M}
.
The Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse of matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
is denoted by
\boldsymbol{M}^{\dagger}
.
The Kronecker product between two matrices
\boldsymbol{U}
,
\boldsymbol{V}
with

\boldsymbol{U}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times q}
is

\boldsymbol{U}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}u_{11}\boldsymbol{V}&\dots&u_{1q}\boldsymbol{V}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
u_{k1}\boldsymbol{V}&\dots&u_{kq}\boldsymbol{V}\\
\end{pmatrix}\text{so that }\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{X}&{\mathbf{0}}_{n\times p}&\dots&{\mathbf{0}}_{n\times p}\\
{\mathbf{0}}_{n\times p}&\boldsymbol{X}&\dots&{\mathbf{0}}_{n\times p}&\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
{\mathbf{0}}_{n\times p}&\dots&\dots{\mathbf{0}}_{n\times p}&\boldsymbol{X}\end{pmatrix}

for
\boldsymbol{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
. We will use the mixed product property
of Kronecker products,

(1.13)

(\boldsymbol{U}\otimes\boldsymbol{V})(\boldsymbol{P}\otimes\boldsymbol{Q})=(\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{P})\otimes(\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{Q}),\qquad(\boldsymbol{U}\otimes\boldsymbol{V})^{\dagger}=\boldsymbol{U}^{\dagger}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}^{\dagger}

whenever the dimensions are such that the matrix products
\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{P}
and
\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{Q}
make sense.
The following trace property also holds

(1.14)

\trace[\boldsymbol{U}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}]=\trace[\boldsymbol{U}]\trace[\boldsymbol{V}].

If
\|\cdot\|
denotes a Schatten norm (e.g., Frobenius or spectral norm), then for any
\boldsymbol{U}
,
\boldsymbol{V}
we have

(1.15)

\|\boldsymbol{U}\otimes\boldsymbol{V}\|=\|\boldsymbol{U}\|\|\boldsymbol{V}\|.

We define the vectorization
\vec(\boldsymbol{U})

of any matrix
\boldsymbol{U}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times q}
by stacking vertically the columns of
\boldsymbol{U}

into a column vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{qm\times 1}
, i.e.,

\vec(\boldsymbol{A})^{\top}=\begin{pmatrix}u_{11}&u_{21}&\dots&u_{m1}&u_{12}&u_{22}&\dots&u_{m2}&\dots&u_{1q}&u_{2q}&\dots&u_{mq}\end{pmatrix}.

For any three matrices
\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B},\boldsymbol{C}
such that the matrix product
\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{C}

makes sense, the above vectorization operator satisfies

(1.16)

\vec(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{B}\boldsymbol{C})=(\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{A})\vec(\boldsymbol{B}).

These many properties of Kronecker products are referenced in Section 4.2 of
[
23
]
.

We consider restrictions of vectors (respectively matrices)
by zeroing the corresponding entries (respectively columns). More precisely,
if
\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
and
B\subset[p]
then
\boldsymbol{v}_{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
is the vector
with
(\boldsymbol{v}_{B})_{j}=0
if
j\notin B
and
(\boldsymbol{v}_{B})_{j}=v_{j}
if
j\in B
.
If
\boldsymbol{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
and
B\subset[p]
,
\boldsymbol{X}_{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}

is a matrix of the same
dimension as
\boldsymbol{X}
such that
(\boldsymbol{X}_{B})\boldsymbol{e}_{j}={\mathbf{0}}
if
j\notin B
and

(\boldsymbol{X}_{B})\boldsymbol{e}_{j}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
if
j\in B
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{X}_{B}
is a copy of

\boldsymbol{X}
after having zeroed the columns not indexed in
B
.
Finally,
I\{\Omega\}
denotes the indicator function of an event
\Omega
,
and
I\{i\in B\}=1
if
i\in B
and
I\{i\in B\}=0
if
i\notin B

is the indicator that some index
i
belongs to
B
.

2
The interaction matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
of the Multi-Task Lasso estimator

We consider the multi-task Lasso estimator, with
\ell_{2,1}
penalty,
given (
1.4
) for some tuning parameter
\lambda>0
.
Let
\hat{S}=\{j\in[p]:{{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}}\neq 0\}
denote the set of nonzero rows of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
. We will refer to
\hat{S}
as the support of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}

and denote by
|\hat{S}|
its cardinality.
The above estimator is the one commonly used in the multi-task learning literature
under a row-sparsity assumption on
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
, see, e.g.,
[
33
]
.
Recall that
\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
is a copy of
\boldsymbol{X}

obtained after zeroing the columns not belonging to
\hat{S}
. Define

{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}

where
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product defined in
Section

1.10
,
so that
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{nT\times pT}
is block-diagonal with
T
blocks,
each equal to
\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}
.
Consequently
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{(pT)\times(pT)}
is also block-diagonal with
T
blocks
equal to
\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}
.
For any
j\in\hat{S}
, define the matrix

(2.1)

\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}\coloneqq\lambda\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}~\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}^{-2}\right)\quad\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times T}

and note that
\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}
is proportional to an orthogonal projection of rank
T-1
.
The matrix
\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}
is the Hessian of
\boldsymbol{u}\mapsto\lambda\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}

at
\boldsymbol{u}={\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
.
Finally, let

{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{(pT)\times(pT)}
be the matrix defined by

{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}\coloneqq\sum_{j\in\hat{S}}\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}\otimes(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top})
.

Definition 1
.

The interaction matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times T}
of the estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}

in (
1.4
)
is defined entrywise by

(2.2)

\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}_{tt^{\prime}}\coloneqq\trace\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}{\mathbf{0}}_{n\times p(t-1)}&\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}&{\mathbf{0}}_{n\times p(T-t)}\end{array}\right]\left[{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}\right]^{\dagger}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathbf{0}}_{p(t^{\prime}-1)\times n}\\[2.84526pt]
\hline\cr\rule{0.0pt}{11.38109pt}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\top}\\[2.84526pt]
\hline\cr{\mathbf{0}}_{p(T-t^{\prime})\times n}\end{array}\right]\right)

for all
t,t^{\prime}\in[T]
, where
\dagger
denotes the Moore-Penrose inverse.
Equivalently, if
\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
then

\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\boldsymbol{v}=\trace\Big(\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}u_{1}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}&u_{2}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}&\dots&u_{T}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}\end{array}\right]\left[{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}\right]^{\dagger}\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}v_{1}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}&v_{2}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}&\dots&v_{T}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}\end{array}\right]^{\top}\Big),

or with Kronecker product notation,

(2.3)

\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\boldsymbol{v}=\trace\big[(\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})[{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}]^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{v}\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{{\top}})\big].

Observe that
\sum_{j\in\hat{S}}(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top})\otimes\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}
is a block-diagonal matrix
with
p
diagonal blocks equal to
I\{j\in\hat{S}\}\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}
.
For
\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}
any
square matrices,
\boldsymbol{A}\otimes\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{P}(\boldsymbol{B}\otimes\boldsymbol{A})\boldsymbol{P}{}^{\top}
holds
for a permutation matrix
\boldsymbol{P}
that only depends on the dimensions
of
\boldsymbol{A}
and
\boldsymbol{B}
.
This permutation
\boldsymbol{P}
is particularly simple and known as a perfect shuffle.
It follows that
\boldsymbol{P}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}\boldsymbol{P}{}^{\top}
is block diagonal with
p
diagonal blocks
for some permutation matrix
\boldsymbol{P}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{pT\times pT}
.
Thus the matrix

(2.4)

{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}\qquad\in{\mathbb{R}}^{pT\times pT}

appearing in (
2.2
)-(
2.3
)
is the sum of two matrices of size
pT\times pT
, each summand
being block diagonal but in a different basis.
If
\lambda=0
then
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}=\mathbf{0}
and
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
is diagonal
as
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}
can be inverted by block.
This corresponds to the unregularized least-squares estimate

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{(ls)}
discussed in (
1.2
)
with
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{(ls)}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}
depending on the
t
-th response
\boldsymbol{y}^{(t)}
only.
In the case
\lambda>0
of interest here, the matrix
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}
induces
nonzero entries outside of the
T
diagonal blocks of
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}
,
the matrix (
2.4
) is not diagonal by block
and the resulting matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
is not diagonal.
Additional structure in (
2.4
) and
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}

is studied in
Section

5
, which yields an efficient
and practical algorithm to compute
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
.

The interaction matrix plays a major role in the construction
of our confidence intervals for
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}

as well as for chi-square inference regions for rows of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
.
A high-level interpretation
of its role is that
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
captures the correlation between the residuals
on different tasks. The following proposition summarizes some useful
properties of
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
.
Result (iii) is important as our confidence interval for
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}

defined in the next section will involve the inverse of
\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n
.

Proposition

2.1
is proved in
Section

B
of the supplement.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
be defined by (
2.2
). Then

(i)

\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
is symmetric and positive semi-definite.

(ii)

If
\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}
is rank
|\hat{S}|

then the spectral norm of
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
is bounded from above as
\|\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\|_{op}\leq|\hat{S}|
.

(iii)

If
\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}
is rank
|\hat{S}|
and
{|\hat{S}|}/{n}<1
then
\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n
is positive-definite
and

\|\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\|_{op}\leq(|\hat{S}|/n)/(1-|\hat{S}|/n)
.

3
Asymptotic normality and confidence intervals in the multi-task setting

3.1
Known
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
: Pivotal random variable, asymptotic normality
and confidence intervals

We assume throughout this section that the direction
\boldsymbol{a}
of interest
is normalized with
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}=1
. This normalization
assumption is relaxed in the next
Section

3.2
where
we develop a methodology for unknown
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
.
If
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is known, our main result is the following
where
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
denotes the interaction matrix (
2.2
).

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
1.1

be fulfilled.
Assume that
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}^{2}=1
. If
\boldsymbol{z}_{0}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}
then

(3.1)

\frac{n\boldsymbol{a}^{T}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{b}+\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{b}}{\|(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{b}\|_{2}}\quad\smash{\xrightarrow{d}}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1)

for any
\boldsymbol{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
.
Hence for
\boldsymbol{b}=\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
, the parameter
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}

of interest satisfies

(3.2)

\frac{n(\boldsymbol{a}^{T}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}-\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)})+\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}}{\|(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\|_{2}}\quad\smash{\xrightarrow{d}}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1).

Theorem

3.1
is proved in
Section

E
.
The left-hand sides of both displays in
Theorem

3.1
can be interpreted
as Z-scores that have asymptotically standard normal distribution.
In the second display, the only unknown quantity on the left hand side
is
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}
, the parameter of interest
(while in the first display, the only unknown quantity is the scalar
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\boldsymbol{b}
).
Consequently if
z_{\alpha/2}
is the
1-\alpha/2
quantile of the standard normal distribution such that
{\mathbb{P}}(|\mathcal{N}(0,1)|\leq z_{\alpha/2})=1-\alpha
,
an asymptotic
1-\alpha
confidence interval for
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}
is given by

[L_{-}^{\alpha},L_{+}^{\alpha}]
where

L_{\pm}^{\alpha}=\underbrace{\boldsymbol{a}^{T}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{initial}\\
\text{estimate}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\frac{\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\frac{\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}}{n})^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}}{n}}_{\text{bias correction using the interaction matrix}}\pm\underbrace{\frac{z_{\alpha/2}\|(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\frac{\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}}{n})^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\|_{2}}{n}}_{\text{confidence interval half-length}}.

(
3.2
) in
Theorem

3.1

states that
{\mathbb{P}}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}\in[L_{-}^{\alpha},L_{+}^{\alpha}])\to(1-\alpha)
as
n,p\to+\infty
.

The confidence interval is centered at

\boldsymbol{a}^{T}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
(which can be interpreted as the initial estimate of
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}

given by the estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
in (
1.4
))
plus a de-biasing correction

\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{T}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}
that involves the interaction
matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
through the matrix inverse

(3.3)

(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}.

The fact that penalized estimators such as (
1.4
) require
a de-biasing correction should be expected since it is already the case
for
T=1
for the Lasso

[
56
,
51
,
26
,
27
,
28
,
5
]
and any regularized least-squares

[
6
]
.
However, the apparition in the de-biasing correction of the interaction matrix
through the matrix inverse (
3.3
) is surprising
at least to us: we did not expect the multi-task de-biasing correction
to require a matrix inversion such as (
3.3
) when initially
tackling this problem.
The length of the confidence interval above is

2z_{\alpha/2}n^{-1}\|(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\|_{2}
when
\boldsymbol{b}=\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
,
and an estimate of this norm is given by the following theorem.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let the assumptions and setting of
Theorem

3.1
be fulfilled.
Then

\|(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{b}\|_{2}^{2}/n\;\smash{\xrightarrow{{\mathbb{P}}}}\;\sigma^{2}

when
\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{2}=1
.

Consequently the length of the confidence interval is approximately

2z_{\alpha/2}\sigma n^{-1/2}
which is the typical length for
two-sided confidence intervals for an unknown mean
\mu
when observing i.i.d.
Y_{1},...,Y_{n}

with
{\mathbb{E}}[Y_{i}]=\mu,\mathrm{Var}[Y_{i}]=\sigma^{2}
.

Theorems

3.1
and
3.2
are proved together in
Section

E
.

Comparison with single-task Lasso on the first task.

It is instructive to compare the above confidence interval with the confidence interval
induced by a single-task Lasso estimator computed on
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{y}^{(1)})
,
i.e., when throwing away the responses
\boldsymbol{y}^{(2)},...,\boldsymbol{y}^{(T)}
on tasks
2,...,T
.
This is also a good opportunity to analyse the form of

\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
and the matrix inversion (
3.3
) in the degenerate case
where a single task is observed.

For
T=1
, a response vector
\boldsymbol{y}^{(1)}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{(1)}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

is observed and
the estimator (
1.4
) reduces to the usual Lasso
with response vector
\boldsymbol{y}^{(1)}
,

\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{L}=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\boldsymbol{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{(1)}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{b}\|^{2}/(2n)+\lambda\|\boldsymbol{b}\|_{1}.

The asymptotic normality result in
Theorem

3.1
for
\boldsymbol{b}=1
asserts that

(3.4)

\frac{n\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{L}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)})+(1-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}_{11}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{y}^{(1)}-\boldsymbol{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{L})}{(1-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}_{11}/n)^{-1}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{(1)}-\boldsymbol{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{L}\|_{2}}\quad\smash{\xrightarrow{d}}\quad\mathcal{N}(0,1).

In the degenerate case
T=1
, the matrices in (
2.1
) are all zeros
and the matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
reduces to a scalar
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}_{11}
equal to

\trace[\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}^{L}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}^{L}})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}]=|\hat{S}^{L}|

where
\hat{S}^{L}
is the support of the Lasso
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{L}
.
Here
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}_{11}
is the usual effective degrees-of-freedom for the Lasso.
The factor
(1-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}_{11}/n)=(1-|\hat{S}^{L}|/n)^{-1}
in (
3.4
) is the
degrees-of-freedom adjustment for the Lasso studied in
[
5
]
,
which is required for the asymptotic normality result (
3.4
)
when

s\gtrsim n^{2/3}

[
5
]
.
So
Theorem

3.1
reduces to the asymptotic normality result of
[
5
]
in the degenerate case
T=1
, and in this case the matrix inversion
(
3.3
) reduces to a degrees-of-freedom adjustment
through the scalar multiplication by
(1-|\hat{S}^{L}|/n)^{-1}
.
The length of the resulting confidence interval for
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}

when
T=1
(or when the tasks
2,...,T
) are thrown away) is then

(3.5)

2z_{\alpha/2}n^{-1}\|\boldsymbol{y}^{(1)}-\boldsymbol{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{L}\|_{2}(1-|\hat{S}^{L}|/n)^{-1}.

We may compare the lengths of the two confidence intervals:

•

The confidence interval
[L_{-}^{\alpha},L_{+}^{\alpha}]
based on (
3.2
) using the responses on all tasks
1,...,T
with
length

2n^{-1}z_{\alpha/2}\|(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\|_{2}
, and

•

The confidence interval based on (
3.4
) obtained by throwing away the responses on tasks
2,...,T
with length (
3.5
).

The length of the confidence interval based on
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
and the responses on all tasks

1,...,T
is smaller than the length (
3.5
) only when

(3.6)

\|\boldsymbol{y}^{(1)}-\boldsymbol{X}\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{}^{L}\|_{2}(1-|\hat{S}^{L}|/n)^{-1}>\|(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\|_{2}.

Our simulations in
Section

6
(see
Figure

5
) reveal
that (
3.6
) holds,
in some situations with significant margins,
when
s
is not too large.
Since the comparison (
3.6
) can be performed
by looking at the data, the practitioner should choose the multi-task confidence interval
based on (
3.2
)
over the single-task confidence interval based on (
3.4
)
when (
3.6
) holds.
When performing this comparison, two tests are constructed which calls for
a Bonferroni correction to avoid invalid coverage due to multiple testing.

3.2
Unknown
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
: Pivotal random variable, asymptotic normality
and confidence intervals

The knowledge of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is not available in most practical situations
and the methodology of the previous subsection cannot be applied.
Indeed the left hand sides in
Theorem

3.1

involve
\boldsymbol{z}_{0}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}
which cannot be directly constructed from
the data when
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
unknown.
Another issue that arises when
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is unknown is that one cannot
verify the normalization
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}=1
required in
Theorem

3.1
.
Intuitively, though, if it was possible to estimate both

\boldsymbol{z}_{0}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}
and
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}
fast enough, replacing
these quantities by their estimates in (
3.2
) should
not break asymptotic normality.
Following ideas from the early de-biasing literature
[
56
,
27
,
51
]
, we consider a direction

(3.7)

\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{e}_{j}

for some fixed covariate
j\in\{1,...,p\}
and compute the nodewise Lasso

(3.8)

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{(j)}=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}/(2n)+\hat{\tau}_{j}(1+\eta)\sqrt{(2/n)\log p}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{1}

for regressing
\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
on
\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}
,
where
\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
is the matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
with
j
-th column replaced
by a column of zeros,

\hat{\tau}_{j}
is a consistent estimate of
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}^{-1}

and
\eta>0
is a small constant.
Alternatively, one may use the scale invariant version of (
3.8
)
again for regressing
\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
on
\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}
,

(3.9)

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{(j)}=\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}:\gamma_{j}=0}\big(\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{2}^{2}/(2n)\big)^{1/2}+(1+\eta)\sqrt{(2/n)\log p}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{1},

known as Scaled lasso
[
48
]

or square-root Lasso
[
7
]
,
and (
3.9
) is equal to (
3.8
)
with
\hat{\tau}_{j}=\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{(j)}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n}
.
We finally set

(3.10)

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{(j)}.

This corresponds to the residuals of the estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{(j)}

in the linear model

(3.11)

\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}=\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(j)}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{(j)}

with response vector
\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
design matrix
\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}
,
true regression vector

\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(j)}\coloneqq-\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}^{-2}(\boldsymbol{I}_{p}-\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}

(so that
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(j)}=0

and
\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(j)}=-(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1})_{jj}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1})_{jk}
for
k\in[p]\setminus\{j\}
),
and Gaussian noise vector

{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{(j)}\coloneqq\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}^{-2}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}

independent of
\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}

with distribution

{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{(j)}\sim\mathcal{N}_{n}(\mathbf{0},\tau_{j}^{2}\boldsymbol{I}_{n\times n})
where
\tau_{j}^{2}\coloneqq\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}^{-2}=(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1})_{jj}^{-1}
.
The relationship between
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}
and
(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(j)},\tau_{j})

is the well known connection between precision matrix
and linear regression for multivariate normal random vectors
(see, e.g.,

[
38
,
49
]
).

The estimators
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{(j)}
in (
3.8
)
and (
3.9
) both satisfy inequalities

(3.12)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{(j)})\|_{\infty}=\|\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}^{\top}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\tau_{j}\sqrt{n\log p},

(3.13)

\displaystyle\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{(j)}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(j)}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\|_{op}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(j)}\|_{0}\tau_{j}\sqrt{\log(p)/n}

provided that
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\|_{op}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(j)}\|_{0}\log(p)/n\to 0
.
Inequality (
3.13
) is the usual

\ell_{1}
estimation rate for the Lasso
[
9
]

or the Scaled Lasso
[
49
,
7
]
, and

\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\|_{op}^{-1}
represents a high-probability
lower bound on the restricted eigenvalue
in the linear model (
3.11
)
[
44
]
.
Inequality (
3.12
) follows from the
KKT conditions of (
3.8
) for the Lasso,
and from the KKT conditions of (
3.9
)
combined with
\hat{\tau}_{j}/\tau_{j}\smash{\xrightarrow{{\mathbb{P}}}}1
which holds thanks to
properties of the Scaled or square root Lasso
[
49
,
7
]
.
Inequalities (
3.12
)-(
3.13
)
are the only properties of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{(j)}
that we will use
in the proof of the following result. Other estimators

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{(j)}
could be used, for instance ones based on
the Dantzig selector, as long as
(
3.12
)-(
3.13
)
are satisfied.

Theorem 3.3
.

Consider a canonical basis direction

\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
for some
j\in[p]
and let
1.1
be fulfilled.
Additionally assume that the sparsity of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
satisfies
either

(3.14)

n^{-1/2}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{0}\sqrt{[T+\log(p/s)]\log p}\to 0.

or

(3.15)

\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{0}\log(p)/n\to 0\qquad\text{ and }\qquad s\sqrt{\log(p)[T+\log(p/s)]/n}\to 0.

Then for any estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{(j)}

satisfying (
3.12
)-(
3.13
)
and every fixed
\boldsymbol{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
we have

(3.16)

\frac{n\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{b}+n({\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j})^{-1}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{b}}{(\tau_{j})^{-1}~\|(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\boldsymbol{b}\|_{2}}~{\xrightarrow{d}}~\mathcal{N}(0,1).

Asymptotic normality (
3.16
) still holds if
\tau_{j}
in the denominator
is replaced by either

({\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}/n)^{1/2}

or
\hat{\tau}_{j}=(\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n})
.

Theorem

3.3

is proved in
Section

G.1
.

4
Confidence ellipsoids for rows of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}

4.1
Known
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

We first construct confidence ellipsoids with the knowledge of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Define the observable positive semi-definite matrix

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}=(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times T}
as well as

(4.1)

\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}_{0}+(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}.

Then
under
1.1
, there exists
a random variable
\chi^{2}_{T}
with chi-square distribution with
T

degrees of freedom such that

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-\tfrac{T}{n}}\Big\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\xi}\Big\|_{2}-\sqrt{\chi^{2}_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)+O_{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\min\Bigl\{\frac{T}{\sqrt{n}},\frac{s^{2}\log^{2}(p/s)}{n\sqrt{T}}\Bigr\}\Big)

as well as

-o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)-O_{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\frac{T}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{sT+s\log(p/s)}{n}\sqrt{T}\Big)\leq\sqrt{1-\tfrac{T}{n}}\Big\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\xi}\Big\|_{2}-\sqrt{\chi^{2}_{T}}.

Consequently,

(i)

(1-\frac{T}{n})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}{}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{2}-(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}\leq o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
holds if additionally

\min\{\frac{T^{2}}{n},\frac{\log^{8}p}{n}\}\to 0
, and

(ii)

(1-\frac{T}{n})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}{}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{2}-(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}\geq o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

holds if additionally
\frac{T^{2}}{n}+\frac{sT+s\log(p/s)}{n}\sqrt{T}\to 0
.

Theorem

4.1

is proved in
Section

F
.
The following proposition with
W_{n}=(1-\frac{T}{n})^{1/2}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}{}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{2}

relates the
(1-\alpha)
-quantile of
\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}{}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{2}

to that of
(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}
when either (i) or (ii) above holds.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
(W_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of random random variables
and
\chi^{2}_{T}
a sequence of random variables
with chi-square distribution with
T
degrees-of-freedom,
where
T=T_{n}
is function of
n
(in particular,
T\to+\infty

as
n\to+\infty
is allowed).
If
\alpha\in(0,1)
is a fixed constant not depending on
n,T

and
q_{T,\alpha}>0
is the quantile defined by

{\mathbb{P}}((\chi_{T}^{2})^{1/2}\leq q_{T,\alpha})=1-\alpha
then

(i)

W_{n}-(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}\leq o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
implies that

{\mathbb{P}}(W_{n}\leq q_{T,\alpha})\geq 1-\alpha-o(1)
and

(ii)

W_{n}-(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}\geq-o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
implies that

{\mathbb{P}}(W_{n}\leq q_{T,\alpha})\leq 1-\alpha+o(1).

Proposition

4.2
is proved in

Section

F
.
If
T\to+\infty
, the order of
q_{T,\alpha}
is given
by

(4.2)

q_{T,\alpha}-\sqrt{T}\to z_{\alpha}/{\sqrt{2}}

where
z_{\alpha}
is the standard normal quantile defined
by
\int_{-\infty}^{z_{\alpha}}(\sqrt{2\pi})^{-1}e^{-u^{2}/2}du=1-\alpha
.
A short proof of (
4.2
)
is given around (
F.17
);
see
[
41
]
for related discussions.
However, using
q_{T,\alpha}
itself to construct confidence sets
should be preferred in practice to avoid the approximation error
in (
4.2
).

Combining the above two results provides confidence ellipsoids for the rows of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
,
or more generally for the unknown vector
(\boldsymbol{B}^{*}){}^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}

for a fixed direction
\boldsymbol{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
of interest.
Let
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
be the subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
defined by

\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}\coloneqq\Big\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}:(1-\tfrac{T}{n})^{1/2}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}{}^{-1/2}\big[(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}_{0}+(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}-\boldsymbol{\theta})\big]\|_{2}\leq q_{T,\alpha}\Big\}.

Since

\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}=\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}:(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{u})^{\top}\boldsymbol{C}(\boldsymbol{\theta}-\boldsymbol{u})\leq 1\}

where

\boldsymbol{C}=(q_{T,\alpha})^{-2}(1-\tfrac{T}{n})(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}){\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}{}^{-1}(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})\text{ and }\boldsymbol{u}={\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}+(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}_{0},

this set is an ellipsoidal region with center
\boldsymbol{u}
.
If

(4.3)

\min\Bigl\{\frac{T^{2}}{n},\frac{\log^{8}p}{n}\Bigr\}\to 0

additionally to
1.1
as required
in case (i) of
Theorem

4.1
,
then
{\mathbb{P}}[(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\in\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}]\geq 1-\alpha-o(1)
.
If additionally

(4.4)

T^{2}/n+\sqrt{T}\bigl(sT+s\log(p/s)\bigr)/n\to 0

as required in case (ii) for the lower bound,
then
{\mathbb{P}}[(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\in\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}]\to 1-\alpha

and the above confidence ellipsoid provides the exact nominal coverage
(i.e., it is provably non-conservative).
Note that the upper bound (i) is more important than the lower bound (ii)
since the upper bound (i) guarantees that the type I error
in the hypothesis test (
1.6
)
is at most

\alpha
, i.e.,
{\mathbb{P}}[(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\in\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}]\geq 1-\alpha-o(1)
.
It is thus fortuitous that only the weak additional condition
(
4.3
) is required for the upper bound (i)
to guarantee the desired type I error, while the more stringent
condition (
4.4
) is only required
to prove non-conservativeness.

The additional assumption (
4.3
)
is satisfied for a large class of growths of
(T,n,p)
.
For instance it holds under polynomial growth
p\asymp n^{\gamma}

or exponential growth of the form

p\lesssim\exp(n^{1/8-\gamma^{\prime}})
for constants
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}>0
,
as
\frac{\log^{8}p}{n}\to 0
is then satisfied.
Although we believe that the mild condition
(
4.3
) is an artefact of the proof,
it is unclear at this point how to relax (
4.3
)
unless a different ellipsoid is considered.
In
Section

4.3
, we will construct a different ellipsoid
that does not require the extra conditions
(
4.3
) or (
4.4
)
but that has worse performance in simulations.

The radius of
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}

i.e., the half-length of its largest axis is given by

(4.5)

\phi_{\min}(\boldsymbol{C})^{-1/2}=(1-T/n)^{-1/2}q_{T,\alpha}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}{}^{1/2}(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})^{-1}\|_{op}.

Since
\|\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}\|_{op}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

by
Proposition

2.1
and
Lemma

C.3
on the one hand,
and all eigenvalues of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}
are of order
\sigma^{2}n(1+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1))

by the arguments in the proof of
Lemma

F.3
on the other hand,
the radius (
4.5
) is
q_{T,\alpha}\sigma n^{-1/2}(1+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1))
which is of order
\sigma\sqrt{T/n}
by (
4.2
).

The random vector (
4.1
) involves multiplication
by
(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})
which differs from the pivotal quantity
in the asymptotic normality result (
3.1
).
However,
Theorem

4.1
still holds with
\boldsymbol{\xi}
in (
4.1
)
replaced by

(4.6)

\check{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}_{0}+n({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}.

Indeed, with

\big|\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}^{-1/2}\check{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\|_{2}-\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{2}\big|\leq\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}^{-1/2}(\check{\boldsymbol{\xi}}-\boldsymbol{\xi})\|_{2}=\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}^{-1/2}\bigl((\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}-\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\bigr)\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{2}.

Since the eigenvalues of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}
are all of order
\sigma^{2}n(1+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1))

,
since
\|(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}-\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\|_{op}\leq(1+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1))|\hat{S}|/n

by
Proposition

2.1

and since
\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{2}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{\sigma^{2}nT})
by
Theorem

F.2
,
the previous display is
O_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{T}s/n)
and converges to 0 in probability
by
1.1
. Under
1.1
,
Theorem

4.1

thus holds for
\boldsymbol{\xi}
in (
4.1
) if and only if it holds
for
\check{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
. Furthermore the corresponding ellipsoid,

\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}=\Big\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}:(1-\tfrac{T}{n})^{1/2}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}{}^{-1/2}\big[(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}_{0}+n({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}-\boldsymbol{\theta})\big]\|_{2}\leq q_{T,\alpha}\Big\}

enjoys the same properties as
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}
:
Type I error guarantees

{\mathbb{P}}[(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\in\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}]\geq 1-\alpha-o(1)

under (
4.3
),
and non-conservativeness

{\mathbb{P}}[(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\in\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}]\to 1-\alpha

under (
4.4
).

4.2
Unknown
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

A similar result is available if
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is unknown.
Consider the notation (
3.10
) from

Section

3.2
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Consider a canonical basis direction

\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
for some
j\in[p]
and let
1.1
be fulfilled.
Additionally assume that either
(
3.14
)
or (
3.15
) holds.
Then for any estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{(j)}

satisfying (
3.12
)-(
3.13
),

(4.7)

\frac{\sqrt{n-T}}{\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}\|_{2}}\Bigl\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}^{-1/2}\Big((\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}+\frac{(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}}{n({\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j})^{-1}}\Big)\Bigr\|_{2}\\
\leq\sqrt{\chi^{2}_{T}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)+O_{\mathbb{P}}(\min\{\tfrac{T}{\sqrt{n}},~\tfrac{\log^{2}p}{n^{1/4}}\})

where
\chi^{2}_{T}
is a random variable with chi-square distribution
with
T
degrees-of-freedom.

Theorem

4.3

is proved in
Section

G.2
.
The corresponding confidence ellipsoid for the
j
-th row

(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
is

\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}^{j}=\Bigl\{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}:\frac{{\sqrt{n-T}}}{\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}\|_{2}}\Bigl\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}^{-1/2}\Big[(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}+\frac{(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j})}{n({\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j})^{-1}}\Big]\Bigr\|_{2}\leq q_{T,\alpha}\Bigr\}.

If either one of the condition
(
3.14
)
or (
3.15
) holds
on the growth of the sparsity of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
,
this confidence ellipsoid does not require the knowledge of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

and has the same guarantees as those of the previous section.

4.3
Relaxing the additional assumptions
(
4.3
) and (
4.4
)

Instead of normalizing using
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}{}^{-1/2}
as in the previous
sections, a simple estimate of
\sigma^{2}
lets us relax
the conditions
(
4.3
) and (
4.4
)
that are required in the previous section to ensure

\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}{}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{2}=(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\xi},\check{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
be defined in (
4.1
) and (
4.6
) respectively,
and let
\hat{\sigma}^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\|_{F}^{2}/(nT)
.
Then under
1.1
,
there exists a random variable
\chi^{2}_{T}
with chi-square
distribution with
T
degrees of freedom such that

(4.8)

(\hat{\sigma}^{2}n)^{-1/2}\|\boldsymbol{\xi}\|_{2}=(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),\qquad(\hat{\sigma}^{2}n)^{-1/2}\|\check{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\|_{2}=(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Theorem 4.5
.

Consider a canonical basis direction

\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
for some
j\in[p]
and let
1.1
be fulfilled.
Additionally assume that either
(
3.14
)
or (
3.15
) holds.
Then for any estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{(j)}

satisfying (
3.12
)-(
3.13
),

(4.9)

\frac{1}{\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}\|_{2}\hat{\sigma}}\Bigl\|(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}+\frac{(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}}{n({\widehat{\boldsymbol{z}}}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j})^{-1}}\Bigr\|_{2}=\sqrt{\chi^{2}_{T}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

where
\chi^{2}_{T}
is a random variable with chi-square distribution
with
T
degrees-of-freedom.

The above asymptotic chi-square results hold under the same
assumptions as
Theorem

3.1
and
Theorem

3.3
.
The reason for the success of these estimates is that

\hat{\sigma}
estimates
\sigma
at a rate faster than
T^{-1/2}
:
we have
|\hat{\sigma}/\sigma-1|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(T^{-1/2})

by
Theorem

F.2
.
However, simulations in
Section

6
reveal that the
asymptotic
(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}
estimates
of the previous subsections involving the matrix
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}}{}^{-1/2}

are more robust to larger sparsity levels, although

1.1
is oblivious to this phenomenon.

The corresponding
1-\alpha
confidence ellipsoid for
(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}

based on (
4.8
) and

\check{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
is

(4.10)

\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\hat{\sigma},\alpha}=\Bigl\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}:\tfrac{1}{\hat{\sigma}\sqrt{n}}\big\|\bigl(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\tfrac{\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}}{n}\bigr)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}_{0}+n({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}-\boldsymbol{\theta})\big\|_{2}\leq q_{T,\alpha}\Bigr\}

and satisfies
{\mathbb{P}}[(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\in\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\hat{\sigma},\alpha}]\to 1-\alpha
under
1.1
.
Similar confidence ellipsoids based on
(
4.9
)
can be readily constructed.

4.4
Hypothesis testing

We now turn to type II error for the testing problem

(4.11)

H_{0}:(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}=\mathbf{0}_{T\times 1}\qquad\text{ against }\qquad H_{1}:\|(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}\geq\rho_{n}

where
\rho_{n}>0
is a separation radius.
The hypothesis test
(
4.11
)
at level
1-\alpha
is naturally achieved by rejecting

H_{0}
if and only if
\mathbf{0}_{T\times 1}\not\in\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\hat{\sigma},\alpha}
for the ellipsoid in (
4.10
).
Similar rejection procedures can be obtained
with
\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}

or
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha}^{j}
for the confidence ellipsoids
defined in
Sections

4.1
and
4.2
.

We can also determine the separation radius
\rho_{n}
required so that
this testing procedure has nontrivial power (type II error).
Focusing here on
\check{\mathcal{E}}_{\hat{\sigma},\alpha}
in
(
4.10
),
rejection happens if and only if the following quantity is positive

\displaystyle(\hat{\sigma}^{2}n)^{-1}\|(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}_{0}+n{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}^{2}-q_{T,\alpha}^{2}

\displaystyle=W_{n}^{2}-q_{T,\alpha}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+(\hat{\sigma}^{2}n)^{-1}\|n(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}^{2}
