\displaystyle\quad+2(\hat{\sigma}^{2}n)^{-1/2}n\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\check{\boldsymbol{\xi}}.

where

W_{n}^{2}=(\hat{\sigma}^{2}n)^{-1}\|\check{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\|_{2}^{2}

and
W_{n}=(\chi^{2}_{T})^{1/2}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
by
(
4.8
).
By
Theorem

3.1
applied to
\boldsymbol{b}=(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\|(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}^{-1}
,
the last line
is of the form
2\hat{\sigma}^{-2}\|(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
so that it is of order

\|(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
.
The second line is positive, of order

\sigma^{-2}n(1+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1))\|(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}^{2}
;
this is the quantity that should dominate in order
to ensure that the above display is positive.
Since the first line

W_{n}^{2}-q_{T,\alpha}=(W_{n}-q_{T,\alpha})(W_{n}+q_{T,\alpha})

is positive with probability at least
\alpha-o(1)

by
Proposition

4.2
, we obtain
that if
\|(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}\geq\rho_{n}
for

\rho_{n}/(\sigma n^{-1/2})\to+\infty
, then
the type II error is at most
1-\alpha+o(1)
.
Although this type II error is typically a constant
close to 1 (e.g. if
\alpha=0.05)
,
this shows that the above test has at most constant type II error
as long as the separation radius satisfies
\rho_{n}\ggg\sigma n^{-1/2}
.
We can also find conditions on
\rho_{n}
that ensures that the type II
error is smaller than any constant. The first line above
is of order
(W_{n}-q_{T,\alpha})O_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{T})=O_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{T})

since
W_{n}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{T})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
and
q_{T,\alpha}=\sqrt{T}+O(1)

by
Proposition

4.2
and
(
4.2
).
Thus

\rho_{n}\ggg T^{1/4}\sigma n^{-1/2}
is sufficient
in order for

(\hat{\sigma}^{2}n)^{-1}\|n(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\|_{2}^{2}
to dominate both the first and third lines with probability
approaching one.
In summary,

\rho_{n}\ggg\sigma n^{-1/2}
is sufficient to achieve a constant
type II error, while

\rho_{n}\ggg T^{1/4}\sigma n^{-1/2}
is sufficient to grant
a vanishing type II error.

In single task models, coefficients
\boldsymbol{B}_{jt}^{*}

of order

o(\sigma n^{-1/2})
cannot be detected, cf.
the discussion after (
1.6
).
Here on the other hand in the multi-task setting with
T\to+\infty
,
detection of non-zero vector

(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
is possible with constant power
even if the individual coefficients in

(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
are

u_{n}\sigma(Tn)^{-1/2}
for any slowly increasing
u_{n}
with
u_{n}\to+\infty
.
If
u_{n}=o(\sqrt{T})
, the coefficients

(\boldsymbol{B}_{jt}^{*})_{t=1,...,T}
are individually impossible
to detect, while detection of the row vector
(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
is possible
with constant type I and type II errors.
Similarly, if the individual coefficients
(\boldsymbol{B}_{jt}^{*})_{t=1,...,T}

are of order

u_{n}\sigma T^{-1/4}n^{-1/2}
for any slowly increasing
u_{n}
with
u_{n}\to+\infty
, the above testing procedure for the row vector

(\boldsymbol{B})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
has vanishing type II error.

5
Computing the interaction matrix efficiently

Equation

2.2
which defines
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
is convenient for theoretical purposes,
as the pseudoinverse suppresses invertibility issues
and the form
(
2.2
) naturally arises in the proofs, see for instance

Lemmas

C.7
,
C.8
and
C.9
.
However, (
2.2
) is not computationally tractable
as it involves computing a pseudoinverse of size
pT\times pT
.
The goal of this section is to provide a computationally
tractable representation for
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
; in particular we will see
that one only needs to compute inverses of matrices of size

|\hat{S}|\times|\hat{S}|
.
A first step when implementing is to remove all covariates
j\in\{1,...,p\}

such that
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}=\mathbf{0}
, as
dropping those indices and the corresponding columns of
\boldsymbol{X}

does not change the value of
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
in (
2.2
).
For the purpose of this section and only in this section,
we assume without loss of generality
that
\hat{S}=[p]
and that all variables
j\in[p]

are such that
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\neq{\mathbf{0}}
.
However, we will keep the notation
\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}

and use summation sign
\sum_{j\in\hat{S}}

to emphasize that the indices
j\notin\hat{S}

and corresponding columns of
\boldsymbol{X}
have been dropped.

Before stating a formal proposition
with a computationally friendly representation of the matrix

\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
, we explain the crux of the argument,
which relies on the
Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury inversion formula.
Recall that

{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}=(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})
and that
for every
j\in\hat{S}

(
2.1
)

\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}\coloneqq\lambda\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}^{-1}\bigl(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}~\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}^{-2}\bigr)\quad\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times T},

as well as
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}=\sum_{j\in\hat{S}}\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}\otimes(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top})
.
By splitting the part of
\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}
proportional to the identity
and the rank one part, we find

\displaystyle{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})+nT\lambda\sum_{j\in\hat{S}}\frac{\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}}{\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}}-\Big[\frac{{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}}{\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}^{3}}\otimes(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top})\Big]

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\big(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v})\big)\right)-nT\lambda\sum_{j\in\hat{S}}\Big[\frac{({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\otimes(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top})}{\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}^{3}}\Big]

where
\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|\hat{S}|}
is the vector with
v_{j}=nT\lambda\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}^{-1}

and
\diag(\boldsymbol{v})
is the square diagonal matrix with
\boldsymbol{v}
as its diagonal.
By the mixed product property
(
1.13
)
we have

({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\otimes(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top})=({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{j})({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{j})^{\top}

so that, with
\boldsymbol{b}^{(j)}=(nT\lambda\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}^{-3})^{1/2}~{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}

we obtain

\displaystyle{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\big(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v})\big)\right)-\sum_{j\in\hat{S}}(\boldsymbol{b}^{(j)}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{j})(\boldsymbol{b}^{(j)}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{j})^{\top}

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\big(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v})\big)\right)-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{M}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top},

where
\boldsymbol{U}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{(|\hat{S}|T)\times|\hat{S}|}
has columns

(\boldsymbol{b}^{(j)}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{j})_{j\in\hat{S}}
and

\boldsymbol{M}=\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\big(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v})\big).

If
\boldsymbol{M}

is invertible and its inverse can be computed efficiently,
the inverse of the above display is given by the
Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula
[
24
]
:
if the matrix

-\boldsymbol{I}_{|\hat{S}|\times|\hat{S}|}+\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}
is invertible then
\boldsymbol{M}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}

is also invertible and

(\boldsymbol{M}-\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top})^{-1}=\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}\left(-\boldsymbol{I}_{|\hat{S}|\times|\hat{S}|}+\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}.

Since
\boldsymbol{v}
has positive entries,

\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v})
is always invertible
and so is
\boldsymbol{M}
, with

(5.1)

\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\big(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v})\big)^{-1}.

Hence we only need to perform two inversions of matrices of size
|\hat{S}|\times|\hat{S}|
: the inversion of
\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v})

and of

-\boldsymbol{I}_{|\hat{S}|\times|\hat{S}|}+\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

With the above notation for

\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|\hat{S}|}
and

\boldsymbol{b}^{(j)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
for each
j\in\hat{S}
,
if the matrix
\boldsymbol{P}
defined entrywise by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}=(P_{jk})_{(j,k)\in\hat{S}\times\hat{S}},\quad P_{jk}=-I\{j=k\}+(\boldsymbol{b}^{(j)}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{b}^{(k)})~(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\big(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v})\big)^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{k})

is invertible then

\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}=\trace\Bigl[{\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v}))^{-1}\Bigr]~\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\Bigl[\sum_{j\in\hat{S}}\boldsymbol{b}^{(j)}\sum_{k\in\hat{S}}~(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{e}_{k})(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{k})\boldsymbol{b}^{(k)}{}^{\top}\Bigr]

where

\boldsymbol{Q}=\big(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v})\big)^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}\big(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v})\big)^{-1}.

Proof.

By definition of
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
and
using the above Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury identity

\displaystyle\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}_{t,t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\trace[(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}})^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top})]

\displaystyle=\trace[(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top})]

\displaystyle\qquad-\trace\bigl[(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}\bigl(-\boldsymbol{I}_{|\hat{S}|\times|\hat{S}|}+\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}\bigr)^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top})\bigr]

\displaystyle=\trace[(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}})\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v}))^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top})]

\displaystyle\qquad-\trace\bigl[\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}((\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top})\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}\bigl(-\boldsymbol{I}_{|\hat{S}|\times|\hat{S}|}+\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}\bigr)^{-1}\bigr].

By (
1.14
), the first term equals

I\{t=t^{\prime}\}\trace[\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v}))^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}]
which gives the first term
in the proposition, proportional to
\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}
.
Using again the structure of
\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}
in (
5.1
),
the second summand in the previous display is equal to

(5.2)

-\trace\bigl[\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\bigl((\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top})\otimes\boldsymbol{Q}\bigr)\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\bigr]

where
\boldsymbol{P}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}
are given in the proposition,
after noting that the definition of
\boldsymbol{P}
is equivalent to

\boldsymbol{P}=-\boldsymbol{I}_{|\hat{S}|\times|\hat{S}|}+\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{M}^{-1}\boldsymbol{U}
.
Since
\boldsymbol{U}
has columns
\boldsymbol{b}^{(j)}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
,
the entry
(j,k)\in\hat{S}\times\hat{S}
of the matrix

\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\bigl((\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top})\otimes\boldsymbol{Q}\bigr)\boldsymbol{U}

is equal to

(\boldsymbol{b}^{(j)}{}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\boldsymbol{b}^{(k)})~(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{e}_{k})=(\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{b}^{(j)})(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\boldsymbol{b}^{(k)})~(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{e}_{k}).

Since
\trace[\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{B}]=\sum_{j,k}A_{jk}B_{jk}
for two symmetric
matrices of the same size, we obtain

(
5.2
)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j\in\hat{S}}\sum_{k\in\hat{S}}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{b}^{(j)})(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\boldsymbol{b}^{(k)})~(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{e}_{k})(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{k})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{e}_{t^{\prime}}^{\top}\Bigl[-\sum_{j\in\hat{S}}\boldsymbol{b}^{(j)}\sum_{k\in\hat{S}}~(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{e}_{k})(\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{P}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{k})\boldsymbol{b}^{(k)}{}^{\top}\Bigr]\boldsymbol{e}_{t}.

On the last line, the matrix in bracket is the second matrix
in the expression of
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
.
∎

We now turn to implementation details. We recommend
an approach that makes use of optimized
vectorized code as often as possible
to compute the quantities in
Proposition

5.1
,
and if available to use a library with Einstein summation routine
as this allows the code to mimick the mathematical notation
in
Proposition

5.1
.
For concreteness, the following code lets us efficiently compute
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}

with the Python library Numpy
[
22
]
,
and the Einstein summation function

numpy.einsum
which comes in handy.
Assume that

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
has been computed, the rows in
[p]\setminus\hat{S}
removed
and the result stored in an array

B_S
of size
|\hat{S}|\times T
,
that
\boldsymbol{X}
with the columns in
[p]\setminus\hat{S}
removed
is stored in an array
X_S
of size
n\times|\hat{S}|
,
and that the scalar
nT\lambda
is stored in variable
nTlambda
.
Then the vector
\boldsymbol{v}

and matrix with columns
(\boldsymbol{b}^{(j)})_{j\in\hat{S}}
in variable
\verb|b|

can be computed as follows:

⬇

import

numpy

as

np

norms

=

np
.
linalg
.
norm
(
B_S
,

axis
=1)

#

shape

(|Ŝ|,

)

v

=

nTlambda

*

norms
**(-1)

#

shape

(|Ŝ|,

)

b

=

nTlambda
**0.5

*

np
.
einsum
(”
j
,
jt
->
jt
”,

norms
**(-3/2),

B_S
)#

shape

(|Ŝ|,

T
)

Finally, matrices
(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}+\diag(\boldsymbol{v}))^{-1}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}

are computed using built-in symmetric matrix inversion,
while computation of
\boldsymbol{P}
and
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
again resorts to using
np.einsum
:

⬇

gram

=

X_S
.
T

@

X_S

#

shape

(|Ŝ|,|Ŝ|)

inverse

=

np
.
linalg
.
invh
(
gram

+

np
.
diag
(
v
))

#

shape

(|Ŝ|,|Ŝ|)

Q

=

inverse

@

gram

@

inverse

#

shape

(|Ŝ|,|Ŝ|)

P

=

-

np
.
eye
(
p
)

+

np
.
einsum
(”
jt
,
kt
,
jk

->

jk
”,

b
,

b
,

inverse
)

#

shape

(|Ŝ|,|Ŝ|)

A

=

np
.
eye
(
T
)

*

np
.
einsum
(”
jk
,
jk
->”,

gram
,

inverse
)

\

-

np
.
einsum
(”
jt
,
ku
,
jk
,
jk
->
tu
”,

b
,

b
,

Q
,

np
.
linalg
.
invh
(
P
))#

shape

(
T
,

T
)

In
einsum
, we use indices
t
and
u
to loop over
[T]
,
and indices
j
and
k
to loop over
\hat{S}
.
All calls to
einsum
can be further optimized
by pre-computing the optimal order in which tensor contractions
should be performed (see
numpy.einsum_path
).

Empirically, we have observed that this implementation
using the Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury identity and the above
code is several orders of magnitude faster than a naive one
involving sparse matrices and the full inversion of

{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}
.

6
Numerical experiments

We run simulations to illustrate the theorems proved in
Sections

3
and
4
.
The values of the parameters are fixed to
n=2000
,
p=6000
,
T=10
,
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}=0
,
\sigma^{2}=1
.
The tuning parameter is

\lambda=\max_{j}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{jj}^{1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\big(1+\sqrt{\frac{2}{T}\log\frac{p}{s}}\big)

(we explain below how
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is constructed).
The directions of interest are
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\boldsymbol{b}=\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}
.

Quantile-quantile plots of the pivotal quantities

The goal is to assess how the sparsity of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
influence the convergence in

Theorems

3.1
,
3.3
,
4.1
,
4.3
and
4.5
.
Denote by
s
and
s_{\Omega}
the respective sparsity parameters that will vary in the experiments.
Given a target tuple
(s,s_{\Omega})
we generate
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
with exactly
s
non-zero rows and

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
with exactly
s_{\Omega}
non-zero entries on the first column of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}
,
so that
s_{\Omega}=\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\|_{0}
.

We explain first how
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is constructed so that it satisfies the constraints in
1.1

as well as the sparsity requirement on
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
.
Start by sampling
\boldsymbol{M}
, a
(p-1)\times(p-1)
matrix with i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries.
Then perform the QR decomposition of
\boldsymbol{M}
to obtain an orthogonal matrix
\boldsymbol{Q}
,
the distribution of which is uniform in the sense of Haar measure on the orthogonal group
O(p-1)
.
Next, consider
\boldsymbol{D}
, the diagonal
(p-1)\times(p-1)
matrix with entries
\{1+j/(p-2):j\in\{0,\ldots,p-2\}\}

and set
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\boldsymbol{Q}\boldsymbol{D}\boldsymbol{Q}^{\top}
.
Define the block matrix

{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}}=\begin{bmatrix}3/2&\hskip-5.0pt\vline\hskip-5.0pt&\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\\
\hline\cr\boldsymbol{v}&\hskip-5.0pt\vline\hskip-5.0pt&\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\end{bmatrix}

where
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p-1}
is a vector with sparsity
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\|_{0}-1
and norm
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}=1
.
This ensures boundedness of the spectrum as the smallest eigenvalue of
{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}}
satisfies the lower bound

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}({\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}})

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{\min}\Big(\begin{bmatrix}3/2&-\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\\
-\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}&\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})\end{bmatrix}\Big)=\frac{5-\sqrt{17}}{4}\gtrsim 0.219,

where the last equality follows from
\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})=1
and
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}=1
.
Similarly, the largest eigenvalue of
{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}}
can be bounded above by

\lambda_{\max}({\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}})=\frac{7+\sqrt{17}}{4}\lesssim 2.8.

Finally set
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\alpha^{-1}{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}}{}^{-1}
where
\alpha
is the greatest diagonal entry of
{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}}{}^{-1}

so that
\max\{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{jj},j=1,...,p\}=1
.
This construction
leads to
\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\approx 0.32,\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\approx 1.76
and

(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1})_{jj}\approx 1.85
.

The row-sparse matrix
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
is constructed as follows.
Initialize
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
as a matrix filled with
\lambda
’s and alter it in two different ways:

(i)

Setting with overlapping supports.

In the first setting, we zero out rows of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
while forcing an overlap of the supports of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}

(either
\supp(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1})\subset\supp(\boldsymbol{B}^{*})
or the
reverse inclusion).
The intuition is that this makes inequality (
G.4
)
tight. This constraint is therefore expected to slow down convergence.

(ii)

No-overlap setting.

In the second setting this constraint is removed and the support
of
\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
is picked uniformly
at random as a subset of

\{1,...,p\}\setminus\supp(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1})
.

Assume that the tuple
(s,s_{\Omega})
is fixed.
We sample
N_{sim}=128
instances of
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{E})
.
For each sample, we compute the estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
using the function
MultiTaskElasticNet
from the Python library Scikit-learn
[
43
]
,
build the interaction matrix
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
using the implementation from
Section

5

and collect the pivotal quantities appearing in the Theorems.
The Q-Q plots and histograms for different pairs
(s,s_{\Omega})
are then reported
in
Figures

1
and
2
for the overlapping supports setting

(i)

and
Figures

3
and
4
for the no-overlap setting

(ii)
.

Asymptotic normality is observed empirically on
Figure

3
in the no-overlap setting,
both when
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is known (blue) and unknown (green).
The convergence holds up well across a wide range of sparsity levels.
In the overlapping supports setting of
Figure

1
, convergence is maintained if
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is known,
but in the unknown
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
case it deteriorates fast when
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\|_{0}
grows.
This suggests
that condition (
3.14
)
is not an artefact of the proof.

The picture is different with chi-square results.
In the no-overlap setting of
Figure

4
,
convergence is observed across all sparsity levels for pivotal quantities
in
Theorem

4.1
(known
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
) and
Theorem

4.3
(unknown
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
)
whereas an increase in
s
slows down convergence in
Theorem

4.5
(unknown
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
).
In the overlapping supports setting
(i)
of
Figure

2
, pivotal quantities in
Theorems

4.1
and
4.5
exhibit the same behavior
as in the previous setting whereas the one from
Theorem

4.3
shows increasingly slower convergence as
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\|_{0}
grows.
Again, this suggests
that condition (
3.14
)
is not an artefact of the proof.

6.1
The advantage of multi-task learning for narrower confidence intervals

In
Figure

5
we illustrate the discussion around (
3.6
)
by comparing the lengths of
95\%
confidence intervals
obtained via multi-task Lasso and single-task Lasso.

\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\|_{0}
is set to
5
and the pair
(T,s)
varies.
For a given
(T,s)
and a sampled
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{E})

we compute the relative change
(\text{length}_{multi}-\text{length}_{single})/\text{length}_{single}
.
We collect these values over
N_{sim}=128
samples and obtain the bottom figure.
Since the results with or without the overlap constraint in the supports are similar, only the no-overlap setting
(ii)
is shown.
In the upper figure,
multi-task confidence interval lengths are pooled together over the samples and we compare them to the aggregate single-task lengths.
As a sanity check we observe that multi-task and single-task Lasso coincide when
T
is equal to
1
.
For
s=15
,
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
-based confidence intervals always have smaller length, which shrinks as
T
increases.
When
T=20
we observe a
40\%
average gain in the width.
Exploiting several tasks thus provides better estimates than intervals based on the first task.
However, as
s
grows, this effect fades gradually and when
s=100
it is counterbalanced by high variance in the multi-task lengths.

:

(blue),

(green).

(orange).

:

is shown and

is set to

.

SUPPLEMENT

A
Intuition

Let us give some rationale behind the pivotal quantities stated in the
main theorems.
In this paragraph and only in this paragraph for the sake of providing
some intuition, we assume that
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
for some
canonical basis vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
and
that
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\boldsymbol{I}_{p\times p}
so that entries of
\boldsymbol{X}
are i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
In this setting, the random vector
\boldsymbol{z}_{0}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
has i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries
and is independent of
\boldsymbol{X}_{-j}
, the matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
with
j
-th column removed.
Since
\boldsymbol{z}\sim\mathcal{N}_{n}({\mathbf{0}},\boldsymbol{I}_{n\times n})
, Stein’s formula

[
45
,
46
]

states
that
{\mathbb{E}}[\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{f}(\boldsymbol{z}_{0})]={\mathbb{E}}[\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\partial/\partial z_{0i})f_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{0})]

for any differentiable vector field
\mathbf{f}=(f_{1},...,f_{n})
with
\mathbf{f}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, under integrability conditions. For the sake of the current informal argument,
assume that Stein’s formula provides reasonable approximation.
Then applying Stein’s formula to
\mathbf{f}=(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\boldsymbol{e}_{t}

for each task
t=1,...,T
(here,
\boldsymbol{e}_{t}
is the
t
-th canonical basis vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
),
by nontrivial computations that are made rigorous in the proofs given in the supplement,
the approximations

(A.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\boldsymbol{e}_{1}

\displaystyle\approx n\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{1}-\sum\nolimits_{t=1}^{T}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}_{1t}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{t},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\boldsymbol{e}_{2}

\displaystyle\approx n\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{2}-\sum\nolimits_{t=1}^{T}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}_{2t}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{t},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\boldsymbol{e}_{T}

\displaystyle\approx n\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{T}-\sum\nolimits_{t=1}^{T}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}_{Tt}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{t}

hold up to smaller order terms, where
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
is the interaction matrix
in
Equation

2.2
. By viewing (
A.1
) as a linear system
with
T
equations and the
T
unknowns
(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{t})_{t=1,...,T}
,
and assuming that solving the linear system maintains the approximations, we obtain
that

\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\\
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{T}\\
\end{pmatrix}\approx\Big(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\Big)^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\boldsymbol{e}_{T}\\
\end{pmatrix}

or equivalently

({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}=\bigl(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\bigr)^{-1}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}_{0}
.
Thus the matrix product of
(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})^{-1}
times
the residuals projected onto
\boldsymbol{z}_{0}

provides us with estimates of the bias of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
on the direction
\boldsymbol{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
.
This informal argument is the crux of the rigorous methodology
developed in the next subsections.
In the sequel, we drop the assumption that
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\boldsymbol{I}_{p\times p}
.
When
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\neq\boldsymbol{I}_{p\times p}
is known as in
Section

3.1
, the score
vector
\boldsymbol{z}_{0}
in (
A.1
) has to be replaced
by a random vector proportional to
\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}
.
When
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is unknown as in
Section

3.2
, the score vector
has to be estimated.

B
Proof of Proposition
2.1

We restate the proposition for convenience.

See
2.1

Proof.

(i)
We have the following equalities:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\trace\big[(\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})[{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{T}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}]^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{v}\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{{\top}})\big]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}\trace\big[(\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})[{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{T}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}]^{\dagger}\big]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{=}}\trace\big[[{{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{T}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}]^{\dagger}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{v}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\top}\big]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iv)}}{{=}}\trace\big[[{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{T}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}]^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{u}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})\big]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(v)}}{{=}}\trace\big[(\boldsymbol{u}\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})[{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{T}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}]^{\dagger}\big]=\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\boldsymbol{u}

where
(i)
follows from (
2.3
),
(ii)
is a consequence of
\trace[\boldsymbol{M}_{1}\boldsymbol{M}_{2}]=\trace[\boldsymbol{M}_{2}\boldsymbol{M}_{1}]
and the mixed product property (
1.13
),
(iii)
and
(v)
follow from
\trace[\boldsymbol{M}]=\trace[\boldsymbol{M}{}^{\top}]
,
(iv)
holds because the pseudoinverse preserves symmetry.

This proves that
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
is symmetric.
Since the pseudoinverse of a positive semi-definite matrix is positive semi-definite as well, we also have

(B.1)

\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\boldsymbol{u}=\|([{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{T}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}]^{\dagger})^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top})\|_{F}^{2}\geq 0

so that
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
is positive semi-definite.

(ii)
Recall that
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}
.
By properties of Gram matrices,
\rank({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}})=\rank({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}})=|\hat{S}|T
, hence by the rank-nullity theorem,
\ker({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}})
has dimension
(p-|\hat{S}|)T
.
By definition of
\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}
, each vector
\boldsymbol{e}_{t}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
is in the kernel of
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}
for
j\notin\hat{S}
and
t\in[T]
, hence in
\ker({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}})
. These
(p-|\hat{S}|)T
vectors are linearly independent, so they form a basis of
\ker({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}})
.

Besides, since
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}=\sum_{k\in\hat{S}}\boldsymbol{H}^{(k)}\otimes(\boldsymbol{e}_{k}\boldsymbol{e}_{k}^{\top})
, the mixed product property of Kronecker products (
1.13
) implies that
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{j})=\mathbf{0}
for
j\notin\hat{S}
and
t\in[T]
, hence
\ker({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}})\subset\ker({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}})
. Since these matrices are positive semi-definite, it is easy to check that the reverse inclusion holds as well, so that
\ker({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}})=\ker({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}})
.

Since
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}
is positive semi-definite,

{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}\preceq{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}
holds in the sense of the positive semi-definite order, and

(B.2)

({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}})^{\dagger}\preceq({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}})^{\dagger}

holds
because the two matrices have the same kernel, see
[
29
]
.
Next, using (
B.1
),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=\|([{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{T}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}]^{\dagger})^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{u}\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\top})\|_{F}^{2}+\trace[(\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})\{[{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{T}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}]^{\dagger}-[{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}]^{\dagger}\}(\boldsymbol{u}\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\top})]

\displaystyle\leq\|([{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{T}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}]^{\dagger})^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{u}\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\top})\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\trace[(\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\dagger})(\boldsymbol{u}\otimes(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\top})]

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}\boldsymbol{u})\trace[\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\top}]

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{u}\|^{2}|\hat{S}|,

where the first inequality follows from (
B.2
)
and the third and fourth line follow respectively from

{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}
and the mixed product
property (
1.13
). The last line stems from
the fact that
\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}

is a projection matrix of rank
|\hat{S}|
when
\rank(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})=|\hat{S}|
.

(iii) Since
\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}
has rank
|\hat{S}|
, we have by (ii) that
\|\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}\|_{op}\leq|\hat{S}|<n
. Since
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}
is positive-semi definite, its spectral norm is its largest eigenvalue, hence all the eigenvalues of
\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n
are
<1
, and
\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}}/n
is positive definite. For any
\boldsymbol{M}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T\times T}
with
\|\boldsymbol{M}\|_{op}<1

we have

(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\boldsymbol{M})^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{M}^{k}
.
By the triangle inequality and the submultiplicativity
of the operator norm,

\|(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\boldsymbol{M})^{-1}-\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\|_{op}\leq\|\boldsymbol{M}\|_{op}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\|\boldsymbol{M}\|_{op}^{k-1}=\|\boldsymbol{M}\|_{op}/(1-\|\boldsymbol{M}\|_{op}).

∎

C
Preliminaries

In this section we develop a series of technical lemmas that will be used
for proving
Sections

3
and
4
.
We consider model (
1.2
) and the estimator
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
from (
1.4
).
Let
\eta_{1}>0
,
\eta_{2}\geq 2
,
\eta_{3},\eta_{4}\in(0,1)
and set

\lambda,\lambda_{0}
as in (
1.8
).
Define the sparsity level

(C.1)

\bar{s}=s\Big(1/T+\frac{4\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\|_{op}(1+\eta_{4})^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}(2+\eta_{2}+1/\sqrt{T})^{2}\Big)\frac{2}{(\lambda/\lambda_{0}-1)^{2}}

and note that
\bar{s}
is of the same order as
s
when the spectrum of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

is bounded away from 0 and infinity as in
1.1
.
Let
\mathcal{C}=\{\boldsymbol{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times T}:\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{2,1}\leq 3\sqrt{s}\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{F}\}
,
\kappa=(1-\eta_{3})\phi_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{1/2}
and define the events

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}=\Big\{\max_{\boldsymbol{U}\in\mathcal{C},\boldsymbol{U}\neq 0}\Big|\frac{\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{U}\|_{F}}{\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{U}\|_{F}\sqrt{n}}-1\Big|{<}\eta_{3}\Big\},\quad\Omega_{2}=\Big\{\sum_{j=1}^{p}(\|\boldsymbol{E}^{T}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}-nT\lambda_{0})_{+}^{2}{<}sn^{2}T\lambda_{0}^{2}\Big\},

\displaystyle\Omega_{3}=\Big\{\max_{B\subset[p]:|B|\leq s+2\bar{s}+1}\Big(\max_{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}:\supp(\boldsymbol{v})\subset B}\Big|\frac{\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}}-1\Big|\Big){<}\eta_{4}\Big\},\quad\Omega_{4}=\Big\{\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{op}{<}\sigma(2\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{T})\Big\}

as well as

(C.2)

\Omega_{*}=\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}\cap\Omega_{3}\cap\Omega_{4}.

Since the only randomness is with respect to
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{E})
, we view
the underlying probability space as
\Omega=({\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p})\times({\mathbb{R}}^{n\times T})

and

\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},\Omega_{3},\Omega_{4},\Omega_{*}
as subsets of
\Omega
so that
\Omega_{i}
occurs
if and only if
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{E})\in\Omega_{i}
for each
i=1,2,3
.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
1.1
be fulfilled.
Then
{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega_{*})\to 1
.

Lemma C.2
.

On
\Omega_{*}
we have:

(i)

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}
,

(ii)

n^{-1/2}\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}\leq(1-\eta_{{3}})\bar{R}
,

(iii)

\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}\leq\bar{R}
,

(iv)

\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{2,1}\leq 3\sqrt{s}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}\leq 3\sqrt{s}\phi_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1/2}\bar{R}
,

(v)

\|\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\|_{F}^{2}\leq 8\sigma^{2}nT+2(1-\eta_{3})^{2}n\bar{R}^{2}
,

where

\bar{R}\coloneqq(1-\eta_{{3}})^{-1}{\kappa}^{-1}{2(1+\eta_{1})(3+\eta_{2})\sigma\max_{j}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{jj}^{1/2}}\sqrt{sT/n}\left(1+\sqrt{{(2/T)\log(p/s)}}\right).

Moreover,
\bar{R}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0
under
1.1
.

Lemma C.3
.

On
\Omega_{*}
, inequality

|\hat{S}|\leq\bar{s}
holds with
\bar{s}
in (
C.1
).

Lemma C.4
.

On
\Omega_{*}
we have
\rank(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})=|\hat{S}|
.

Lemma C.5
.

For almost every
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{E})
, the KKT conditions of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
in (
1.4
) hold strictly in the sense that

{\mathbb{P}}(\max_{j\notin\hat{S}}\|(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}<nT\lambda)=1
.

Lemma C.6
.

Given the noise matrix
\boldsymbol{E}
and two design matrices
\boldsymbol{X},{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}
define
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
in (
1.4
)
and
{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}}
by

{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}}={\textstyle\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\boldsymbol{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}}}\Big(\tfrac{1}{2nT}\|\boldsymbol{E}+{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}(\boldsymbol{B}^{*}-\boldsymbol{B})\|_{F}^{2}+\lambda\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{2,1}\Big).

If
\boldsymbol{X},{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}},\boldsymbol{E}
are such that
both
\{(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{E}),({\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}},\boldsymbol{E})\}\subset\Omega_{*}

then

\displaystyle n^{1/2}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq(\eta_{4})(\bar{R}+\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{op}n^{-1/2})\|(\boldsymbol{X}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\|_{F},

\displaystyle\|{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}({\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})-\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq(\eta_{4})(\bar{R}+\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{op}n^{-1/2})\|(\boldsymbol{X}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\|_{F}

for some constants that depend on
\eta_{4}
only
and
\bar{R}
is defined in
Lemma

C.2
.

Lemma C.7
.

For almost every
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{E})
in the open set

\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}\cap\Omega_{3}
,

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
is a Fréchet differentiable function of
\boldsymbol{X}
.
For almost every
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{E})
in

\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}\cap\Omega_{3}
, if

{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}(\boldsymbol{w})={\textstyle\mathop{\rm arg\,min}_{\boldsymbol{B}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}}}\Big(\tfrac{1}{2nT}\|\boldsymbol{E}+(\boldsymbol{X}+\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top})(\boldsymbol{B}^{*}-\boldsymbol{B})\|_{F}^{2}+\lambda\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{2,1}\Big)

is the estimate (
1.4
) with
\boldsymbol{X}
replaced
by the perturbed design
\boldsymbol{X}+\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}
,
then for any
\boldsymbol{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}

\Big((\boldsymbol{X}+\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top})({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}(\boldsymbol{w})-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\Big)\boldsymbol{b}-\Big(\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\Big)\boldsymbol{b}=\big(\mathsf{D}(\boldsymbol{b})\big)\boldsymbol{w}+o(\|\boldsymbol{w}\|)

as
\|\boldsymbol{w}\|\to 0
,
where
\mathsf{D}:{\mathbb{R}}^{T}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
is a linear map given by

\mathsf{D}(\boldsymbol{b})=\mathsf{D}^{*}(\boldsymbol{b})+\mathsf{D}^{**}(\boldsymbol{b})

with

\displaystyle\mathsf{D^{*}}(\boldsymbol{b})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{b})\boldsymbol{I}_{n\times n}-(\boldsymbol{b}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{{\hat{S}}})\big({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}\big)^{\dagger}\big((({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a})\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\big)

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{b})\boldsymbol{I}_{n\times n}-(\boldsymbol{b}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{{\hat{S}}})\left({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}\right)^{\dagger}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{1}\boldsymbol{X}_{{\hat{S}}}^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{T}\boldsymbol{X}_{{\hat{S}}}^{\top}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\mathsf{D^{**}}(\boldsymbol{b})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{b}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{{\hat{S}}})\big({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}\big)^{\dagger}((\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{a}_{\hat{S}})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{b}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{{\hat{S}}})\left({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}\right)^{\dagger}\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{a}_{{\hat{S}}}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{{\hat{S}}}\boldsymbol{e}_{T}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\end{pmatrix}

for all
\boldsymbol{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
and
\boldsymbol{w}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Note that
\mathsf{D},\mathsf{D}^{*}
and
\mathsf{D}^{**}
implicitly depend on
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{E})
.
Hence the matrix
\mathsf{D}(\boldsymbol{b})
of size
n\times n

is the Jacobian of the map

\boldsymbol{w}\mapsto(\boldsymbol{X}+\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top})({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}(\boldsymbol{w})-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{b}

at
\boldsymbol{w}=\mathbf{0}
.

Lemma C.8
.

For any
\boldsymbol{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}

we have on
\Omega_{*}

(C.3)

\displaystyle\trace[\mathsf{D}^{*}(\boldsymbol{b})]

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{b}^{\top}(n\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}-\smash{\widehat{\mathbf{A}}})({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a},

(C.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Big(\trace[\mathsf{D}^{**}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t})]\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\sigma^{2}sT

for some constant depending on
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}

and
\eta_{1},...,\eta_{4}
only.

Lemma C.9
.

Under
1.1
, as
n,p\to+\infty
we have

\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}n}{\mathbb{E}}\Big[I\{\Omega_{*}\}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Big(\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{t}-\trace[\mathsf{D}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t})]\Big)^{2}\Big]\longrightarrow 0.

Since
\Omega_{*}
has probability approaching one, this implies that

\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}n}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\boldsymbol{z}_{0}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{e}_{t}-\trace[\mathsf{D}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t})])^{2}
converges to 0 in probability.

We now prove each lemma. The lemmas are restated before their proofs for convenience.

See
C.1

Proof of
Lemma

C.1
.

The fact that
\Omega_{*}=\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}\cap\Omega_{3}\cap\Omega_{4}
has probability
approaching one under
1.1
follows
from the propositions in
Section

D
:

Proposition

D.1

(iii)
with
k={9s}
and
{x=\log n}
,

Proposition

D.2
,

Proposition

D.3
applied with
k=s+2\bar{s}+1
,
and

{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega_{4})\geq 1-e^{-n/2}
by
[
18
, Theorem II.13]
.
∎

See
C.2

Proof of
Lemma

C.2
.

In the whole proof we place ourselves on the event
\Omega_{*}
. We prove first that
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}
.

By the definition of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
,

\frac{1}{nT}\|\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{Y}\|_{F}^{2}+2\lambda\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\|_{2,1}\leq\frac{1}{nT}\|\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}-\boldsymbol{Y}\|_{F}^{2}+2\lambda\|\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{2,1}
.
Rewriting the LHS as
\frac{1}{nT}\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})+(\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}-\boldsymbol{Y})\|_{F}^{2}+2\lambda\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\|_{2,1}
and expanding the square yields

\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}^{2}\leq 2\langle\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}),\boldsymbol{E}\rangle_{F}+2nT\lambda(\|\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{2,1}-\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\|_{2,1})
.

The following chain of inequalities holds

\displaystyle\langle\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}),\boldsymbol{E}\rangle_{F}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}\cdot\|(\boldsymbol{X}^{T}\boldsymbol{E})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\|({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}\left[(\|\boldsymbol{E}^{T}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}-nT\lambda_{0})_{+}+nT\lambda_{0}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{p}(\|\boldsymbol{E}^{T}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}-nT\lambda_{0})_{+}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}+nT\lambda_{0}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{2,1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iv)}}{{\leq}}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}\sqrt{s}n\sqrt{T}\lambda_{0}+nT\lambda_{0}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{2,1}.

(i)
and
(iii)
follow from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
(ii)
stems from the inequality
a\leq(a-b)_{+}+b
and
(iv)
holds on
\Omega_{2}
.
Thus

(C.5)

\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}^{2}\leq 2\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}\sqrt{s}nT\lambda_{0}+2nT\big[\lambda_{0}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{2,1}+\lambda(\|\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{2,1}-\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\|_{2,1})\big].

Besides, the quantity inside the bracket on the right hand side satisfies

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}{}}\lambda_{0}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{2,1}+\lambda(\|\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{2,1}-\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\|_{2,1})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}\lambda_{0}\sum_{j\in S}\|({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}+\lambda_{0}\sum_{j\notin S}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}+\lambda\Big(\sum_{j\in S}\|{\boldsymbol{B}^{*}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}-\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}\Big)-\lambda\sum_{j\notin S}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}\lambda_{0}\sqrt{s}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}+\lambda_{0}\sum_{j\notin S}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}+\lambda\sum_{j\in S}\|(\boldsymbol{B}^{*}-{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}-\lambda\sum_{j\notin S}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)\sqrt{s}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}+(\lambda_{0}-\lambda)\sum_{j\notin S}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2},

where
(ii)
follows from Cauchy-Schwarz and the reverse triangle inequality applied respectively on the first and third summands of
(i)
, whereas
(iii)
is a consequence of Cauchy-Schwarz.
Combining this bound with (
C.5
) and plugging in the value
\lambda=(1+\eta_{2})\lambda_{0}
yields

(C.6)

\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}^{2}\leq 2nT\sqrt{s}\lambda_{0}\Big(2+\eta_{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\Big)\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}-2nT\eta_{2}\lambda_{0}\sum_{j\notin S}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}.

Non-negativity of the LHS, the equality

\sum_{j\notin S}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}=\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{2,1}-\sum_{j\in S}\|({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}
and Cauchy-Schwarz lead to

\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{2,1}\leq(1+\frac{3}{\eta_{2}}+\frac{1}{\eta_{2}\sqrt{T}})\sqrt{s}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}
.

Since
T\geq 1
and
\eta_{2}\geq 2
, we get
\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{2,1}\leq 3\sqrt{s}\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}
, that is
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}
.

The inequality

(C.7)

\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\|_{op}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}=\phi_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1/2}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}

combined with (
C.6
) and the event
\Omega_{1}
yields

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq 2\kappa^{-1}\Big(2+\eta_{2}+T^{-1/2}\Big)\sqrt{n}T\lambda_{0}\sqrt{s}

\displaystyle\leq{2{\kappa^{-1}}(1+\eta_{1})(3+\eta_{2})\sigma\max_{j}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{jj}^{1/2}}\sqrt{sT}\Big(1+\sqrt{{(2/T)\log(p/s)}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}(1-\eta_{3})\bar{R}.

Reusing
\Omega_{1}
, we obtain

\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}\leq({\sqrt{n}(1-\eta_{3})})^{-1}{\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}}\leq\bar{R}
.

Combining this last bound with (
C.7
) yields

\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}\|_{F}\leq\phi_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-1/2}\bar{R}
, hence (iv).

For inequality (v), using
\Omega_{4}
,
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
and
\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\rank(\boldsymbol{E})\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{op}^{2}
we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{F}^{2}+2\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\rank(\boldsymbol{E})\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{op}^{2}+2n(1-\eta_{3})^{2}\bar{R}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\min(n,T)(2\sigma\max(\sqrt{n},\sqrt{T}))^{2}+2n(1-\eta_{3})^{2}\bar{R}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 8\sigma^{2}nT+2(1-\eta_{3})^{2}n\bar{R}^{2}.

Regarding the limit of
\bar{R}
, note that
\bar{R}\propto\big(\frac{sT}{n}\big)^{1/2}+\big(\frac{s}{n}\log(\frac{p}{s})\big)^{1/2}
.
By
1.1
, each summand goes to
0
as
n
goes to
\infty
.
∎

See
C.3

Proof of
Lemma

C.3
.

The KKT conditions of (
1.4
)
are given by

(C.8)

\begin{split}(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}&=nT\lambda\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}^{-1}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\qquad\text{ for all }j\in\hat{S},\\
\|(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}&\leq nT\lambda\qquad\text{ for all }j\notin\hat{S}.\end{split}

This implies that

\forall j\in\hat{S},\|(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}=nT\lambda.

Since
\|(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}+\|(\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{B}^{*}-{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}))^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}

by the triangle inequality, we have for any
j\in\hat{S}
,

(C.9)

\displaystyle nT\lambda

\displaystyle\leq(\|\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}-nT\lambda_{0})_{+}+nT\lambda_{0}+\|(\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{B}^{*}-{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}))^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2},

(C.10)

\displaystyle nT(\lambda-\lambda_{0})

\displaystyle\leq(\|\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}-nT\lambda_{0})_{+}+\|(\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{B}^{*}-{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}))^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}.

Summing the squares of the above inequalities for a subset
B\subset\hat{S}

and using
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
,
we get

\frac{|B|n^{2}(\lambda-\lambda_{0})^{2}T^{2}}{2}\leq\sum_{j\in B}(\|\boldsymbol{E}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}-nT\lambda_{0})_{+}^{2}+\trace(\{\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{B}^{*}-{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\}^{\top}\Big\{\sum_{j\in B}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}^{{\top}}\Big\}\{\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{B}^{*}-{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\}).

The first term is bounded from above by
sn^{2}T\lambda_{0}^{2}
on the event
\Omega_{2}
. Dividing by
n^{2}T^{2}
we find

\frac{|B|(\lambda-\lambda_{0})^{2}}{2}\leq s\lambda_{0}^{2}/T+\frac{1}{nT^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}^{2}\psi_{\max}(B)

where
\psi_{\max}(B)
is the largest eigenvalue of

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j\in B}\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}^{{\top}}
,
or equivalently the largest eigenvalue of

\frac{1}{n}(\boldsymbol{X}_{B}\boldsymbol{X}_{B}^{{\top}})
, which is also the largest eigenvalue of
\frac{1}{n}(\boldsymbol{X}_{B}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{B})
.
On the event of
\Omega_{*}
, we obtain

\frac{|B|(\lambda-\lambda_{0})^{2}}{2}\leq s\lambda_{0}^{2}/T+\frac{\psi_{\max}(B)}{nT^{2}}\frac{4}{\kappa^{2}}(2+\eta_{2}+1/\sqrt{T})^{2}nT^{2}\lambda_{0}^{2}s,

or equivalently

\frac{|B|(\lambda/\lambda_{0}-1)^{2}}{2}\leq s\Big(1/T+\frac{4\psi_{\max}(B)}{\kappa^{2}}(2+\eta_{2}+1/\sqrt{T})^{2}\Big).

Let
\bar{s}
be as in (
C.1
)
and assume that
|\hat{S}|\leq\bar{s}
is violated on
\Omega_{*}
.
Then on
\Omega_{3}
, any

B\subset\hat{S}
with size
|B|=\lfloor\bar{s}\rfloor+1
satisfies

\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
\|\boldsymbol{X}_{B}\boldsymbol{v}_{B}\|_{2}\leq(1+\eta_{4})\sqrt{n}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\|_{op}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{B}\|_{2}
. Squaring yields

\psi_{\max}(B)\leq\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\|_{op}{(1+\eta_{4})^{2}}
.
Then

\frac{|B|(\lambda/\lambda_{0}-1)^{2}}{2}\leq s\Big(1/T+\frac{4\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\|_{op}{(1+\eta_{4})^{2}}}{\kappa^{2}}(2+\eta_{2}+1/\sqrt{T})^{2}\Big)

which shows that
|B|\leq\bar{s}
by definition of
\bar{s}
, a contradiction.
∎

See
C.4

Proof.

By
Lemma

C.3
, we have
|\hat{S}|\leq\bar{s}
on
\Omega_{*}
. Since
s\leq s+2\bar{s}+1
, the event
\Omega_{3}
yields
\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
\supp(\boldsymbol{v})\subset\hat{S}\implies(1-\eta_{4})\sqrt{n}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}
. If
\boldsymbol{v}
is such that
\supp(\boldsymbol{v})\subset\hat{S}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}\boldsymbol{v}=\mathbf{0}
, then we must have
\boldsymbol{v}=\mathbf{0}
. Equivalently, the linear span of
(\boldsymbol{e}_{j})_{j\in\hat{S}}
has intersection
\{\mathbf{0}\}
with
\ker(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})
, hence
\ker(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})
must be contained in the span of
(\boldsymbol{e}_{j})_{j\notin\hat{S}}
. Thus
\dim\ker(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})\leq p-|\hat{S}|
and by the rank-nullity theorem,
\rank(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})\geq|\hat{S}|
. By definition of
\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}
, it is also clear that
\rank(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})\leq|\hat{S}|
, hence the conclusion.
∎

See
C.5

Proof of
Lemma

C.5
.

This follows from the argument in Lemma 6.4 of

[
5
, arXiv version v1, 24 Feb 2019]
.
∎

See
C.6

Proof of
Lemma

C.6
.

By
Lemma

C.3
,
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}}
has at most
2\bar{s}
non-zero rows.

\Omega_{3}
applied on each column of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})
gives
(1-\eta_{4})^{2}n\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}^{2}
. Similarly, using
\Omega_{3}
with
{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}
and summing the resulting inequality with the previous one yields

2(1-\eta_{4})^{2}n\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}^{2}+\|{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}^{2}.

Define
\varphi:\boldsymbol{B}\mapsto\frac{1}{2nT}\|\boldsymbol{E}+\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{B}^{*}-\boldsymbol{B})\|_{F}^{2}+\lambda\|\boldsymbol{B}\|_{2,1}
,
\psi:\boldsymbol{B}\mapsto\frac{1}{2nT}\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B})\|_{F}^{2}
and
\gamma:\boldsymbol{B}\mapsto\varphi(\boldsymbol{B})-\psi(\boldsymbol{B})
. When expanding the squares, it is clear that
\gamma
is the sum of a linear function and of the convex penalty, thus
\gamma
is convex. Additivity of subdifferentials yields
\partial\varphi({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})=\partial\gamma({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})+\partial\psi({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})=\partial\gamma({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})
. By optimality of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
we have
{\mathbf{0}}_{p\times T}\in\partial\varphi({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})
, thus
{\mathbf{0}}_{p\times T}\in\partial\gamma({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})
. This implies
\gamma({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\leq\gamma({\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})
. Letting
{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}}={\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{H}={\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
, the last inequality rewrites as

\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{X}{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}}\|_{F}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{H}\|_{F}^{2}+g({\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})-g({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}).

Summing the similar inequality obtained by replacing
\boldsymbol{X}
with
{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}
yields

\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}^{2}+\|{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{X}{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}}\|_{F}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{H}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{E}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}\boldsymbol{H}\|_{F}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{E}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}}\|_{F}^{2}.

Combining the above displays, we obtain

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\leq{}}2(1-\eta_{4})^{2}n\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}^{2}+\|{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{X}{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}}\|_{F}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{H}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{E}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}\boldsymbol{H}\|_{F}^{2}-\|\boldsymbol{E}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{H}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}}),2\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{H}+{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}})\rangle_{F}+\langle{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}({\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}}-\boldsymbol{H}),2\boldsymbol{E}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}(\boldsymbol{H}+{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}})\rangle_{F}\quad{\scriptstyle\text{ thanks to }\langle a-b,a+b\rangle_{F}=\|a\|_{F}^{2}-\|b\|_{F}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle(\boldsymbol{X}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}})(\boldsymbol{H}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}}),2\boldsymbol{E}\rangle_{F}+\langle\boldsymbol{H}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}},({{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}-\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}})(\boldsymbol{H}+{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}})\rangle_{F}

\displaystyle=\langle\boldsymbol{H}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}},2(\boldsymbol{X}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{E}\rangle_{F}+\langle\boldsymbol{H}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}},\big[{{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}({\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}-\boldsymbol{X})+({\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}-\boldsymbol{X})^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}}\big](\boldsymbol{H}+{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}})\rangle_{F}.

The second summand rewrites as
\langle{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}(\boldsymbol{H}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}}),({\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}-\boldsymbol{X})(\boldsymbol{H}+{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}})\rangle_{F}+\langle({\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}-\boldsymbol{X})(\boldsymbol{H}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}}),\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{H}+{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}})\rangle_{F}.

By Cauchy-Schwarz and the submultiplicativity of the Frobenius norm, the second summand is bounded above by

\|{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}(\boldsymbol{H}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}})\|_{F}\|(\boldsymbol{X}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\|_{F}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{H}+{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}})\|_{F}+\|(\boldsymbol{X}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\|_{F}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{H}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}})\|_{F}\|\boldsymbol{X}(\boldsymbol{H}+{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}})\|_{F}.

Since
\boldsymbol{H}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}}={\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}}
and
\boldsymbol{H}+{\overline{\boldsymbol{H}}}={\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}+{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}}-2\boldsymbol{B}^{*}
have respectively at most
2\bar{s}
and
2\bar{s}+s
non-zero rows, using
\Omega_{3}
twice gives the following bound on the second summand:

2(1+\eta_{4})\sqrt{n}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}\|(\boldsymbol{X}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\|_{F}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}+{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}}-2\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}.

Combining the above displays, we find

\displaystyle 2(1-\eta_{4})^{2}n\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 2\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}\|(\boldsymbol{X}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\|_{F}\big(\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{op}+(1+\eta_{4})\sqrt{n}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}+{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}}-2\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}\big).

Thanks to
Lemma

C.2

we have
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}\leq\bar{R}

and
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}\leq\bar{R}
, this shows that

\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}\leq{\|(\boldsymbol{X}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\|_{F}\big(\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{op}n^{-1}+2(1+\eta_{4})n^{-1/2}\bar{R}\big)(1-\eta_{4})^{-2}}
, hence

{n^{1/2}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}\leq 2(1+\eta_{4})(1-\eta_{4})^{-2}(\bar{R}+\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{op}n^{-1/2})\|(\boldsymbol{X}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\|_{F}.}

We also have by the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\|{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}({\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})-\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\|{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}({\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}+\|({\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}}-\boldsymbol{X})({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\eta_{4})n^{1/2}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\overline{\boldsymbol{B}}}-{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})\|_{F}+\|(\boldsymbol{X}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\|_{{F}}\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq{\|(\boldsymbol{X}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\|_{F}\Big[2(1+\eta_{4})(1-\eta_{4})^{-2}(\bar{R}+\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{op}n^{-1/2})+\bar{R}\Big]}

\displaystyle\leq{4(1+\eta_{4})(1-\eta_{4})^{-2}\big(\bar{R}+\|\boldsymbol{E}\|_{op}n^{-1/2}\big)\|(\boldsymbol{X}-{\overline{\boldsymbol{X}}})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\|_{F},}

where the last line follows from the inequality
2(1+\eta_{4})(1-\eta_{4})^{-2}\geq 2
for
\eta_{4}\in(0,1)
.
∎

See
C.7

Proof of
Lemma

C.7
.

By
Lemma

C.6
and Rademacher’s theorem, we know that
the Fréchet derivative of
{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
with respect to
\boldsymbol{X}
exists almost everywhere,
so that
\mathsf{D}(\boldsymbol{b})
exists for almost every
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{E})\in\Omega_{*}
.
By
Lemma

C.5
, we also have that for almost every
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{E})
,
the KKT conditions are strict in the sense given in
Lemma

C.5
.
In the following, we consider
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{E})\in\Omega_{*}
such that

\mathsf{D}(\boldsymbol{b})
exists and such that the KKT conditions are strict;
almost every
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{E})\in\Omega_{*}
satisfy these two conditions.

Since we know that the Jacobian
\mathsf{D}(\boldsymbol{b})
exists by Rademacher’s theorem,
it is enough to characterize its value, for instance by
computing the directional derivative in any fixed direction
\boldsymbol{w}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
To this end, for a real
u
in a neighborhood of
0
,
let
\boldsymbol{X}(u)=\boldsymbol{X}+u\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}

and
\boldsymbol{B}(u)={\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}(u\boldsymbol{w})
. Define the active set

\hat{S}(u)=\{j\in[p]:\|\boldsymbol{B}(u)^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}>0\}
.
We also write
\dot{\boldsymbol{X}}=(d/du)\boldsymbol{X}\big|_{u=0}=\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}
,
and
\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}=(d/du)\boldsymbol{B}(u)\big|_{u=0}
.
At
0
, we have
\boldsymbol{X}(0)=\boldsymbol{X}
and
\boldsymbol{B}(0)={\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}
is the estimator computed
at
(\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})
with
\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{B}^{*}+\boldsymbol{E}
.

As in (
C.8
),
the KKT conditions for
\boldsymbol{B}(u)

read, for
j\in\hat{S}(u)
(i.e.,
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}(u)\neq\mathbf{0}
),

\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}(u)^{\top}\big[\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{X}(u)(\boldsymbol{B}(u)-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\big]=\frac{nT\lambda}{\|\boldsymbol{B}(u)^{\top}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\|_{2}}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}(u)\qquad\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1\times T}

and for
j\notin\hat{S}(u)
(i.e.,
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{B}(u)=\mathbf{0}
),

\|\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}(u)^{\top}\big[\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{X}(u)(\boldsymbol{B}(u)-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\big]\|_{2}<nT\lambda.

By Lipschitz continuity of
u\mapsto\boldsymbol{B}(u)
established in
Lemma

C.6
,
the set
\hat{S}(u)
is constant in a neighborhood of 0 because
the KKT conditions on
\hat{S}(u)^{c}
are bounded away from
nT\lambda

on a neighborhood of 0 by continuity, and because
the nonzero rows of
\boldsymbol{B}(u)
are bounded away from
{\mathbf{0}}
in a neighborhood of

0
again by continuity of
\boldsymbol{B}(u)
.
Differentiation of the above display for
j\in\hat{S}(u)
at

u=0
and the product rule yield

\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\Big[\dot{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}))-\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}(\dot{\boldsymbol{X}}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})+\boldsymbol{X}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}})\Big]=nT\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}

with
\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}
in (
2.1
). Rearranging
and using
\dot{\boldsymbol{X}}=\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}
,

\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\Big[\boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}))-\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}))\Big]=\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\Big[nT\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}+\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}\Big]\qquad\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1\times T}.

Let
\boldsymbol{P}_{\hat{S}}=\sum_{j\in\hat{S}}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times p}
.
Multiplying by
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
to the left and summing over
j\in\hat{S}
, we obtain

\boldsymbol{P}_{\hat{S}}\Big[\boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}))-\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}))\Big]=\boldsymbol{P}_{\hat{S}}\Big[nT\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}+\boldsymbol{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}\Big]\qquad\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}.

Since
\hat{S}(u)
is locally constant for
u
in a neighborhood of 0,
we have
\boldsymbol{P}_{\hat{S}}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}=\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}

thus
\boldsymbol{X}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}=\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}
,
hence

\boldsymbol{a}_{\hat{S}}\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{E}-\boldsymbol{X}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}))-\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*}))=nT\Big[\sum_{j\in\hat{S}}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}\Big]+\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\quad\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times T}.

We now use the relationship between vectorization
and Kronecker product (
1.16
).
Applying (
1.14
)
to the previous display for each term, we find

\displaystyle((\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{a}_{\hat{S}})\vec(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top})-\Big(\big(({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\big)\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\Big)\vec(\boldsymbol{w})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Big(\Big[nT\sum_{j\in\hat{S}}(\boldsymbol{H}^{(j)}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}^{\top})\Big]+\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}\Big)\vec(\dot{\boldsymbol{B}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\Big({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}\Big)\vec(\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}).

Since
\vec(\cdot)
is always a column vector,
\vec(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top})=\vec(\boldsymbol{w})=\boldsymbol{w}
.
Finally, we have again using (
1.16
) and the chain rule,
for any fixed
\boldsymbol{b}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}(\boldsymbol{b})\boldsymbol{w}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{d}{du}\boldsymbol{X}(u)(\boldsymbol{B}(u)-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{b}~\big|_{u=0}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{B}(0)-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{b}+\boldsymbol{X}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}\boldsymbol{b}

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{w}\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{B}(0)-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{b}+\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}\boldsymbol{b}}.

By
Lemma

C.4
,
\rank(\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})=|\hat{S}|
.
The argument developed in the proof of
Proposition

2.1
(ii) shows that the nullspace of the matrix
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}

is exactly the linear span of
\{\boldsymbol{e}_{t}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{j},~(j,t)\in\hat{S}^{c}\times[T]\}
.
Because
\boldsymbol{P}_{\hat{S}}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}=\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}
,

\vec(\dot{\boldsymbol{B}})
is in
\ker({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}})^{\perp}=\range({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}})
.
Since for any symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
,
\boldsymbol{M}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{M}

is the orthogonal projection on the range of
\boldsymbol{M}
,
we have
\big(nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}+{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}\big)^{\dagger}\big(nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}+{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}\big)\vec(\dot{\boldsymbol{B}})=\vec(\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}).

Since
\boldsymbol{X}_{{\hat{S}}}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}\boldsymbol{b}
is a column vector, using (
1.16
)
again,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{{\hat{S}}}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}\boldsymbol{b}

\displaystyle=\vec(\boldsymbol{X}_{{\hat{S}}}\dot{\boldsymbol{B}}\boldsymbol{b})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{b}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{{\hat{S}}})\vec(\dot{\boldsymbol{B}})

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{b}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{{\hat{S}}})\big({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}\big)^{\dagger}\Big[((\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{a}_{\hat{S}})-\Big(\big(({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a}\big)\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\Big)\Big]\boldsymbol{w}.

Since this holds for all
\boldsymbol{w}
, this provides the desired
expression for
\mathsf{D}(\boldsymbol{b})
for all
\boldsymbol{b}
.
∎

See
C.8

Proof of
Lemma

C.8
.

For the first equality,

\trace[\mathsf{D^{*}}(\boldsymbol{b})]=\trace[(\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})\boldsymbol{b})\boldsymbol{I}_{n\times n}-(\boldsymbol{b}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{{\hat{S}}})\big({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}{}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}\big)^{\dagger}\big((({\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}-\boldsymbol{B}^{*})^{\top}\boldsymbol{a})\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}^{\top}\big)]

and the conclusion follows from (
2.3
).

For (
C.4
), the following bounds will be useful.
Inequality
\|\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}\|_{op}^{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\|_{op}(1+\eta_{4})^{2}n

holds on
\Omega_{*}
.
Furthermore since
\ker\boldsymbol{N}=\ker\boldsymbol{N}^{\dagger}

for all symmetric matrices
\boldsymbol{N}

and since
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}}
is positive semi-definite,
on
\Omega_{*}
we find

\displaystyle\|({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}})^{\dagger}\|_{op}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big[\min_{\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{np}:\boldsymbol{u}\in\ker({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}})^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}})\boldsymbol{u}\big]^{-2}

\displaystyle\leq\big[\min_{\boldsymbol{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{np}:\boldsymbol{u}\in\ker({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}})^{\perp}}\boldsymbol{u}^{\top}({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}})\boldsymbol{u}\big]^{-2}

(C.11)

\displaystyle\leq{\phi_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-2}(1-\eta_{4})^{-4}n^{-2}}.

We now work on

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\trace[\mathsf{D}^{**}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t})]^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{2}
,
the left hand side of (
C.4
).
For brevity, define
\boldsymbol{M}=({\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{\top}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{X}}}+nT{\tilde{\boldsymbol{H}}})^{\dagger}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes\boldsymbol{a}_{\hat{S}})(\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}})^{\top}.

Then if
\boldsymbol{e}_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
and
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
denote canonical basis vectors,

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\trace[\mathsf{D}^{**}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t})]^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{2}

where
\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{T}
has components
\boldsymbol{v}_{t}=\trace[\mathsf{D}^{**}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t})]

so that

\boldsymbol{v}_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}[\mathsf{D}^{**}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t})]\boldsymbol{e}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}=\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}))\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{e}_{i},

where the last equality stems from two applications of
the mixed product property
(
1.13
):

\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})=(1\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top})(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})=(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top})\otimes(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})=(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T})\otimes(1(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}))\\
=\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})).

Thus

\boldsymbol{v}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{I}_{T\times T}\otimes(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}))\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
and since

\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}=\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{v}=(\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\otimes 1)\boldsymbol{v}
,
it follows that

\|\boldsymbol{v}\|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\otimes(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}}))\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}=\trace[(\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\otimes\boldsymbol{X}_{\hat{S}})\boldsymbol{M}]
by (
1.13
).

By the definition of
\boldsymbol{M}
,
using the commutation property of the trace
we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}
